The Floer Map
We now want to deﬁne the “Floer map”
F : P1,p(x, y) −→Lp(x, y)
by setting
F(u) = ∂u
∂s + J(u)∂u
∂t + grad Ht(u).
Here, Lp(x, y) is a ﬁber bundle on P1,p(x, y) whose ﬁber at u is the space
Lp(u⋆TW) that can be deﬁned in a manner analogous to W 1,p(u⋆TW). By
the deﬁnition of P1,p(x, y), its elements u can be written as u = expw(X) for
a w ∈C∞(R × S1; W) with exponential decay and X ∈W 1,p(w⋆TW). We
therefore need to verify that F takes on its values in Lp, that is, that
F(expw X) ∈Lp(R × S1; Rm),
which we will do in Section 13.3 (Lemma 13.3.1 and Remark 13.3.2).
8.3 The Space of Perturbations of H
We want to show that, arbitrarily close (in the sense of the C∞topology)
to a ﬁxed Hamiltonian H, there exists a Hamiltonian with exactly the same
periodic orbits and for which the M(x, y) are manifolds of the expected di-
mension.

Let us ﬁx a (time-dependent) Hamiltonian H0 whose periodic orbits we
assume to be nondegenerate. The space of “perturbations” of H0 that we
are going to use is the space C∞
ε (H0) of maps h : W × S1 →R such that

PDF PAGE 254

8 Linearization and Transversality
∥h∥ε < +∞and
h(x, t) = 0
in a neighborhood of the 1-periodic orbits of H0.
Since the support of h is “far” from the periodic solutions of H0, at least
when ∥h∥ε is suﬃciently small, the periodic solutions of H0 + h are exactly
the periodic solutions of H0 itself—and they are nondegenerate.
8.4 Linearization of the Floer Equation: Computation of
the Diﬀerential of F
In order to spare beginners the painful diﬀerentiations of vector ﬁelds and the
use of the Levi-Civit`a connection, we will use an embedding of the manifold W
in Rm (m is very large, in any case larger than the dimension 2n of the
manifold). With
TW ⊂Rm × Rm

F is considered to have values in a vector space of maps with values in Rm,
namely C∞(R × S1; Rm) or Lp(R × S1; Rm). To compute the diﬀerential
of F, we therefore ﬁx a solution

This is equivalent to
S−= −J0B−,
and this proves our assertion.

Remark 8.4.7. If u is a solution of the Floer equation, then we have already
said (Remark 6.5.12) that the product u · s deﬁned by
(u · s)(σ, t) = u(σ + s, t)
is also a solution. Consequently, F(u · s) = 0 for every s and therefore
0 = d
dsF(u · s) = (dF)u
∂u
∂s

,
so that ∂u/∂s is a solution of the linearized equation. In particular, along a
(nonconstant) solution connecting x to y, the kernel of (dF)u has dimension
greater than or equal to 1.
8.5 The Transversality
We now prove the transversality property (Proposition 8.1.4), using the Fred-
holm property for (dF)u, that is, modulo the ﬁrst part of Theorem 8.1.5. The
almost complex structure J is ﬁxed, as is the nondegenerate Hamiltonian H0.
We ﬁx an h0 ∈C∞
ε (H0) and a solution u of the Floer equation for J and
H = H0 + h0:
F(u) = ∂u
∂s + J ∂u
∂t + gradu(H) = 0.
The diﬀerential of F is computed, as we have just explained, in a ﬁxed unitary
trivialization along u; we write it as
(dF)u(Y ) = ∂Y
∂s + J0
∂Y
∂t + S · Y.
8.5.a Deﬁnition of Γ
We ﬁx two distinct critical points x and y ∈Crit AH0. To show that Z(x, y, J)
is a manifold, we describe it as the set of zeros of a section of a ﬁber bundle.

On the space P(x, y) × C∞
ε (W, H0), we deﬁne the vector bundle
E = {(u, h, Y ) | Y ∈Lp(u⋆TW)} .
We can view E as a ﬁber bundle
E −→P(x, y) × C∞
ε (H0)
(through (u, h, Y ) 7→(u, h)). The map
P(x, y) × C∞
ε (H0)
σ
−−→E
(u, h) 7−→∂u
∂s + J(u)∂u

in E, that is, the fact that
∂u
∂s + J ∂u
∂t + gradu(H)
is in Lp(R × S1; u⋆TW), as we have already noted in Section 8.2.d, is proved
in Chapter 13 (more precisely, in Lemma 13.3.1 and Remark 13.3.2).
The zeros of σ are exactly the points of Z(x, y, J) (more exactly, of its
W 1,p version); note that the data (H0, J) are now C∞while (u, Y ) are
weak solutions. To conclude, it suﬃces to show that σ is transversal to the
zero section, that is, that the linearized map (dσ)(u,h) composed with the
projection onto the ﬁber is surjective when σ(u, h) = 0.
Note that σ is linear in h and that its diﬀerential is
(dσ)(u,h)(Y, η) = (dF)u(Y ) + gradu(η).
The map
Γ : W 1,p(R × S1; R2n) × C∞
ε (H0) −→Lp(R × S1; R2n),
whose surjectivity is claimed by Proposition 8.1.4, is the composition of
(dσ)(u,h) and the projection from Tσ(u,h)E onto the tangent space of E at
the ﬁber in (u, h). Consequently, Proposition 8.1.3 follows immediately from
Proposition 8.1.4. Note that the trivialization Zi of TW along u allows us to
identify the tangent space of the ﬁber with the space Lp(R × S1; R2n).

Lemma 8.5.3. If Z ∈Lq(R × S1; u⋆TW) is of class C∞and satisﬁes

Proof. The proof relies on a theorem of Floer, Hofer and Salamon that guar-

notion whose details we will give in the deﬁnitions that follow and in Theo-
rem 8.5.4; we will prove the property in Section 8.6.
Let u ∈M(x, y, J, H). A point (s0, t0) ∈R × S1 is called a critical point
of u if ∂u/∂s(s0, t0) = 0. A point (s0, t0) is regular if it is not critical
and if u(s0, t0) ̸= u(s, t0) for every s ̸= s0 (even if s = ±∞, that is, if
u(s0, t0) ̸= x(t0) or u(s0, t0) ̸= y(t0)). The set of critical points is denoted by
C(u) ⊂R × S1. We let R(u) denote the set of regular points of u.
Theorem 8.5.4 ([32]). The set C(u) of critical points is discrete and the
set R(u) of regular points is a dense open subset of R × S1.
We will prove this theorem in Section 8.6.

We can now apply the Sard–Smale theorem, which is as follows.
Theorem 8.5.7 (Sard–Smale [73]). Let E and F be two separable Banach
spaces, let U be an open subset of E and let
F : U −→F
be a (smooth) Fredholm map. Then the set
{y ∈F | if y = f(x) for an x ∈U, then TxF is surjective}
of regular values of F is a countable intersection of dense open subsets.
Remark 8.5.8. In this theorem, the separability guarantees that we can
extract a countable subcover from any open cover. It is essential in order to
obtain a countable intersection of dense open subsets, and therefore a dense
subset.
Let us now prove the following result.
Lemma 8.5.9. The regular values of π are exactly the h ∈C∞
ε (H0) such that

Deﬁnition. We set v(±∞, t) = x±(t). Let (s, t) ∈R2. We call a multiple

do not admit multiples.
The “injectivity” result that we have in view is the following.
Proposition 8.6.3. Let v be a solution of class C∞of the Cauchy–Riemann

Proof. We divide the proof into two steps. We show:
• That R(v) is an open subset, which is quite easy since the two conditions
occurring in the deﬁnition are obviously open conditions
• And (this is the delicate part) that R(v) is dense.

8.6.b The Continuation Principle
We must now prove the continuation principle. We will consider the case of
a “perturbed Cauchy–Riemann” equation
∂Y
∂s + J0
∂Y
∂t + S · Y = 0
in which J0 is the standard complex structure on R2n and
S : R2 −→End(R2n)
is a map of class C∞. Here is the proposition that we are leading up to.
Proposition 8.6.6 (Continuation Principle). Let Y be a solution of the
perturbed Cauchy–Riemann equation on an open subset U of R2. Then the
set
C = {(s, t) ∈U | Y has an inﬁnite-order zero at (s, t)}
is open and closed in U. In particular, if U is connected and if Y is zero on
a nonempty open subset of U, then Y is identically zero on U.
Remarks 8.6.7.
(1) Recall that a function f has an inﬁnite-order zero at a point z0 if, for every
k ≥0,
lim
r→0
sup|z−z0|≤r |f(z)|
rk
= 0.
For a function f of class C∞, this is equivalent to the derivatives of all
orders of f being zero at z0.
(2) The set of points where a function of class C∞has an inﬁnite-order zero
is closed. In the case of a holomorphic function (that is, one satisfying the
Cauchy–Riemann equations), this closed set is also open: this is what we
call the “analytic continuation principle”.
We will prove this proposition by presenting it as a consequence of a version
of the Carleman similarity principle, namely the following lemma. We write
R2n = Cn.
Lemma 8.6.8 (Similarity Principle). Let Y : Bε →Cn be a solution of
class C∞of the perturbed Cauchy–Riemann equation
∂Y
∂s + J0
∂Y
∂t + S · Y = 0

8.6 The Solutions Are “Somewhere Injective”
275
and let p > 2. Then there exist a number δ > 0, δ < ε, a map A ∈
W 1,p(Bδ, GL(R2n)) and a holomorphic map σ : Bδ →Cn such that
∀(s, t) ∈Bδ,
Y (s, t) = A(s, t)σ(s + it) and J0A(s, t) = A(s, t)J0.

PDF PAGE 291
8.7 The Fredholm Property
281
We take inspiration from the proof given in [66] (see also [16]). The main
ingredient is the following elliptic regularity theorem, together with a conse-
quence of the Calder´on–Zygmund inequality (Theorem C.5.8) stated in the
next lemma (a proof can be found in Section 12.2).
Theorem (Theorem 12.1.3). Let p > 1. If Y ∈Lp(R×S1; R2n) is a weak
solution of the linearized Floer equation LY = 0, then Y ∈W 1,p(R×S1; R2n)
(and Y is of class C∞).
Lemma 8.7.2. Let p > 1. If Y ∈W 1,p(R × S1; R2n), then there exists a
positive constant C such that
∥Y ∥W 1,p ≤C(∥LY ∥Lp + ∥Y ∥Lp).
The proof is set up as follows: we ﬁrst consider the case where S(s, t) does
not depend on s, where we prove the following stronger result.
Proposition 8.7.3. If the symmetric operator S(s, t) = S(t) is independent
of s and if det(Id −R±
1 ) ̸= 0, then
D = ∂+ S(t) : W 1,p(R × S1; R2n) −→Lp(R × S1; R2n)
is bijective (for every p > 1).
We ﬁrst prove this proposition in the case p = 2 in Section 8.7.a, and then
in the general case in Section 8.7.b. The invertibility of the “asymptotic”
operator D allows us to improve the inequality of Lemma 8.7.2 to
∥Y ∥W 1,p(R×S1;R2n) ≤C(∥Y ∥Lp(R×S1) + ∥Y ∥Lp([−M,M]×S1))
(for a suﬃciently large M > 0).
Since the restriction W 1,p(R × S1) →Lp([−M, M] × S1) is a compact
operator (by Rellich’s theorem (Theorem C.4.10)), we can then use a gen-
eral result on so-called “semi-Fredholm” operators (whose proof is given in
Appendix C.2.c).
Proposition 8.7.4. Let E, F and G be three Banach spaces. Let L : E →F
be an operator and let K : E →G be a compact operator. We suppose that
there exists a constant C > 0 such that
∀x ∈E,
∥x∥E ≤C (∥L(x)∥F + ∥K(x)∥G) .
Then the kernel of L is ﬁnite-dimensional and its image is closed.

To simplify, let us denote this Hilbert space by H:
H = L2(S1; R2n).
The map A is not deﬁned on H, but on its subspace W 1,2(S1), it is what

domain of A is dense in H = L2(S1) (because C∞(S1) ⊂W 1,2(S1) is dense)
and that A is a closed operator (that is, that its graph

This operator is continuous (because A−1 is continuous by the Banach theo-

Moreover, it is self-adjoint, which easily follows from the similar property
of A. We can therefore apply the following result (which is [17, Section 6.4])
to it.
Theorem 8.7.8. Let H be a separable Hilbert space and let T : H →H be a
compact self-adjoint operator. Then H admits a Hilbertian basis made up of
eigenvectors of T.
Recall that the set of eigenvalues of the operator A,
{λ ∈C | A −λ Id is not injective} ,
is contained in the spectrum of A, which, in turn, consists of the λ for which
A−λ Id is not bijective. For a compact operator, the set of eigenvalues either
is ﬁnite or consists of a sequence that tends to 0 (see [17, Section 6.3]). Note
that in our case, 0 is not an eigenvalue because A−1 is injective. Also note
that the eigenvectors of T are in W 1,2(S1; R2n) because Tv = λv implies
that
λv = A−1v ∈W 1,2(S1; R2n).
We have a decomposition
H = H+ ⊕H−
(as a sum of positive and negative eigenspaces of T, respectively) and two
orthogonal projections p± : H →H±. Let A± be the restriction of A to H±.
Each of these is again an unbounded operator. If Y ∈W 1,2(S1) is in the
eigenspace corresponding to the eigenvalue λ of T, then
AY = 1
λY.
So the operators A+ and −A−are indeed monotone operators (each of them
satisﬁes ⟨Bx, x⟩≥0)
A± : H± −→H±.

Remarks 8.7.10. We called this result surprising for the following reasons:
(1) It is not a local existence result for solutions of a diﬀerential equation:
nothing is said in the neighborhood of 0.
(2) The initial condition is not assumed to be in the deﬁnition space of A (it
is true that the latter is dense in H by the previous remark).
(3) It is, however, a global result, as in the case of a linear ordinary diﬀerential
equation, in the sense that it gives solutions on all of [0, +∞[.
(4) Similarly, as soon as we ﬁx s > 0, the map Y0 7→Y (s) is linear (by
the uniqueness of the solution), deﬁning a continuous linear map from H
to D(A). Let us call it Γ(s). Again by the uniqueness, we have
Γ(s1 + s2) = Γ(s1) · Γ(s2)
and, since Y (0) = Y0, Γ(0) = Id. We say that Γ is a semiﬂow (or a
semigroup). . . and use the notation Γ(s) = e−As.
Our operators A+ and −A−therefore generate “semigroups” of operators

The following result is a consequence of this.
Proposition 8.7.14. The operator D is bijective for p > 2.
Proof. The inequality
∥Y ∥W 1,p(R×S1) ≤C ∥DY ∥Lp(R×S1)
of the previous lemma holds for every Y ∈W 1,p(R×S1)∩W 1,2(R×S1) and,
in particular, for every Y ∈C∞
0 (R×S1). Since the C∞functions with compact
support are dense10 in W 1,p, the inequality remains true for Y ∈W 1,p(R×S1)
(entirely). Hence D is injective. Moreover, since W 1,p is complete, its image
is closed.
To prove that D is also surjective, it suﬃces to establish that its image is
dense in Lp(R × S1). Let Z ∈Lp(R × S1) ∩L2(R × S1). Note that because
this space contains the characteristic functions, it is dense in Lp(R×S1). The
surjectivity of D when p = 2, established earlier, gives a Y ∈W 1,2(R × S1)
such that DY = Z. By the above, this Y is in W 1,p(R × S1), proving our
proposition.
⊓⊔

following result.

conclusion, namely that its kernel is ﬁnite-dimensional.
Let us now show that Coker L is ﬁnite-dimensional. The Hahn–Banach
theorem allows us to ﬁnd linear forms ϕ : Lp →R that are zero on Im L; we
want to prove that the space of these forms is ﬁnite-dimensional. The Riesz
representation theorem allows us to write a linear form as
ϕ = ϕU
for a U ∈Lq with ϕU(V ) = ⟨U, V ⟩.
Since these forms are zero on Im L, the U that represent them must be in F.
But this is ﬁnite-dimensional. Consequently, so is Coker L, concluding the
proof of Proposition 8.7.1.

8.8 Computing the Index of L
To compute the index of L,
L(Y ) = ∂Y
∂s + J0
∂Y
∂t + S(s, t)Y,
with lims→±∞S(s, t) = S±(t) uniformly in t, we will:
(1) Replace L by L0 given by the same formula. . . except that S(s, t) is re-
placed by a matrix eS(s, t) that is exactly S−(t) for s ≤−σ0 and S+(t)
for s ≥σ0. The invariance of the index under small perturbations (for σ0
suﬃciently large) will imply that the index of L0 is the same as that of L.
This is Lemma 8.8.4.
(2) Replace L0 by L1, still given by the same formula, but where eS(s, t) is
replaced by a diagonal matrix S(s) (that does not depend on t and) that
will be constant for s ≤−σ0 and s ≥σ0. This time L1 will have the same
index as L0 because of the invariance of the index under homotopy (and
the construction of limit diagonal matrices). This is Propositions 8.8.2
and 8.8.3.
The process is sketched in Figure 8.7, which represents the diﬀerent maps
S(s, t) that occur in the operators L, L0 and L1 in the space of matrices: the
outer “cylinder” is S(s, t) (the original one), the thick cylinder is eS(s, t) (we
have approximated S at inﬁnity by true cylinders), the dotted line consisting
of the diagonal matrices represents the matrix S(s) (for L1).

Remark 8.8.8. We have replaced the Fredholm operator L by an operator
that has the same index. . . and whose index we know how to compute, be-
cause we can describe its kernel and cokernel. It goes without saying that
there is no reason for the dimensions of these two subspaces to be those of
the kernel or cokernel of L: it is the index that is invariant.

for |s| suﬃciently large and for a constant δ > 0. The function f therefore

Lemma 8.9.4. If f ′′(s) ≥δ2f(s) for s ≥s0, then:
• Either lims→+∞f(s) = +∞
• Or f(s) ≤f(s0)e−δ(s−s0) for s ≥s0.
Proof. Let
g(s) = e−δs(f ′(s) + δf(s)),
so that
g′(s) = e−δs(−δ(f ′(s) + δf(s)) + f ′′(s) + δf ′(s))
= e−δs(f ′′(s) −δ2f(s))
≥0,
by the assumption on f.
In particular, the function g is increasing on [s0, +∞[.
Let us ﬁrst suppose that there exists an s1 ≥s0 with g(s1) > 0. Then
g(s) ≥g(s1) for s ≥s1 and
f ′(s) + δf(s) ≥eδsg(s1)
for s ≥s1. Let h(s) = eδsf(s), then
h′(s) = eδs(δf + f ′) ≥e2δsg(s1),
hence h(s) ≥Ae2δs for s ≥s1 and f(s) ≥Aeδs tends to +∞.
If such an s1 does not exist, then we have g(s) ≤0 for s ≥s0, hence also
f ′(s) + δf(s) ≤0 and h(s) = eδsf(s) is decreasing on [s0, +∞[. Moreover,
eδsf(s) = h(s) ≤h(s0) = eδs0f(s0),
that is,
f(s) ≤eδs0f(s0)e−δs,
which concludes the proof of the lemma.

Chapter 9
Floer Homology: Spaces of Trajectories
We now consider a generic (in the sense of Theorem 8.1.1) Hamiltonian H on
a symplectic manifold W satisfying Assumptions 6.2.1 and 6.2.2 (for example
because π2(W) = 0 or because W is the cotangent bundle of a manifold V ).
We consider the periodic orbits of ﬁxed Maslov index. Let Ck(H) denote
the vector space over Z/2 with basis the periodic orbits of Maslov index k. We
know that these orbits are ﬁnitely many because the nondegeneracy assump-
tion ensures that they are isolated. We deﬁne the diﬀerential of the complex
using the ﬂow of the gradient of the action functional AH,
∂: Ck(H) −→Ck−1(H)
∂(x) =
X
Ind(y)=k−1
n(x, y)y,
with
where n(x, y) denotes the number (modulo 2) of trajectories of the gradient
of AH (that is, of solutions of the Floer equation) connecting x to y. The fact
that n(x, y) is well deﬁned (and ﬁnite) will follow from the fact that under
the assumptions we made, the space L(x, y) of trajectories connecting x to y
is a compact manifold of dimension 0 (see Section 8.5).
We then still need to prove that we have deﬁned a complex, that is, that
∂◦∂= 0. To do this, as in Section 3.2.c, we need to study the space M(x, y)
in more detail when the diﬀerence of the indices is 2.
317

PDF PAGE 328
318
9 Spaces of Trajectories
9.1 The Spaces of Trajectories
9.1.a The Space L(x, y) and Its Topology
Recall that if x and y are critical points of AH, then M(x, y) denotes the
space of (contractible) solutions (of ﬁnite energy) connecting x to y. We have
shown (Theorem 6.5.6) that the space M of contractible solutions of ﬁnite
energy of the Floer equation is the union of the spaces M(x, y).
Let us return to the action of R on M used in the previous proofs. Let
L(x, y) = M(x, y)/R
be the space of trajectories connecting x to y (which we assume to be
distinct). It is endowed with the quotient topology. In this topology, a se-
quence eun converges to an element eu if and only if there exists a sequence sn
of real numbers such that un(sn + s, ·) →u(s, ·) in M(x, y).
Remark 9.1.1. We distinguish between a (parametrized) solution, an ele-
ment of M, and a (nonparametrized) trajectory, an element of L.
Proposition 9.1.2. Let x and y be two distinct critical points of AH and let
un ∈M(x, y), sn ∈R, σn ∈R. Suppose that
lim un(sn + s, ·) = u ∈M(x, z)
and
lim un(σn + s, ·) = v ∈M(x, w)
for two critical points z and w distinct from x. Then z = w and u and v
coincide up to the action of R; in other words, there exists an s⋆such that
u(s⋆+ s, t) = v(s, t).
Corollary 9.1.3. The quotient topology on L(x, y) is Hausdorﬀ.
In fact, Proposition 9.1.2 gives the uniqueness of the limit of a convergent
sequence in L(x, y).
⊓⊔
Proof of Proposition 9.1.2. Since x ̸= y and un ∈M(x, y), AH(x) > AH(y)
and likewise for z and w. We choose a real number α such that
AH(x) > α > sup(AH(y), AH(z), AH(w))
and an ε > 0 such that
AH(x) −ε > α.
We ﬁrst suppose that the sequence σn −sn is not bounded. We may, and
do, assume that sn = 0 and that the sequence σn is not bounded from above.

PDF PAGE 329
9.1 The Spaces of Trajectories
319
It then has a subsequence that tends to +∞. At s = −∞, v departs from the
critical point x, so there exists an s⋆such that
s ≤s⋆=⇒AH(v(s, t)) > AH(x) −ε.
But un(s⋆+σn, ·) tends to v(s⋆, ·), hence, for n suﬃciently large, we also have
AH(un(s⋆+ σn, ·)) > AH(x) −ε.
The solution u tends to z for s →+∞, so there exists an s1 such that
s ≥s1 =⇒AH(u(s, ·)) < α.
Since un(s1, ·) →u(s1, ·), we have, for n suﬃciently large,
AH(un(s1, ·)) ≤α.
It follows that
s⋆+ σn < s1
for every n, contradicting the fact that σn is not bounded from above.
Consequently, σn −sn is bounded and therefore admits a convergent sub-
sequence, say lim(σn −sn) = s⋆. For s ﬁxed, the sequence s+σn −sn remains
within a ﬁxed compact set. Hence un(s+σn, ·) converges to v(s, ·) on the one
hand and to u(s + s⋆) on the other.
⊓⊔
Remark 9.1.4. This proof still holds for u ∈M(x0, z) and v ∈M(x0, w) for
a critical point x0 of AH with AH(x) ≥AH(x0).
Remark 9.1.5. In Morse theory, to show that the spaces of trajectories of a
pseudo-gradient ﬁeld are Hausdorﬀ, we studied the intersection with a level
set of the function. Here we also have
L(x, y) ∼= Mα(x, y) = {u ∈M(x, y) | AH(u(0)) = α} ,
still using the fact that AH is decreasing on the trajectories. Note that to
obtain the latter, we need Theorem 8.5.4. We also know that
Mα(x, y) × R −→M(x, y)
(x, σ) 7−→u(s + σ, t)
is a diﬀeomorphism.
Remark 9.1.6. If x = y, then M(x, y) consists of the constant solution (in s)
equal to x. If x ̸= y, then L(x, y) is a manifold of dimension µ(x) −µ(y) −1

9 Spaces of Trajectories
(the action of R is free by the previous remark). If µ(x) = µ(y) but x ̸= y,
then M(x, y) is empty and so is L(x, y).
9.1.b What We Need to Do To Deﬁne the n(x, y) and To Show
That ∂◦∂= 0
Let us consider the case of two critical points with indices whose diﬀerence is:
• Equal to 1: then L(x, y) is a compact (which we still need to prove) man-
ifold of dimension 0 and n(x, y) = #L(x, y)
• Equal to 2, µ(x) = µ(z) + 2; then L(x, z) is a manifold of dimension 1: we
consider
L(x, z) = L(x, z) ∪
S
µ(y)=µ(x)+1
L(x, y) × L(y, z)
and prove that it is a manifold of dimension 1, whose boundary is the
union of the L(x, y) × L(y, z), and that it is compact.
Once we have proved this, since the boundary of a manifold of dimension 1
consists of an even number of points, we have
X
µ(y)=µ(x)+1
n(x, y)n(y, z) ≡0
mod 2,
that is, ∂◦∂= 0.
We must therefore prove:
• The compactness of the space L(x, y) (Section 9.1.c)
• The fact that L(x, y) is a manifold with boundary when the diﬀerence of
the indices is 2 (Section 9.2).
9.1.c Broken Trajectories
As we saw in Section 3.2 in the ﬁnite-dimensional case, in the closure of
the trajectories connecting the critical point x to the critical point y, there
are “broken” trajectories, which are unions of trajectories connecting other
critical points. The following theorem describes this behavior more precisely.
Theorem 9.1.7. Let (un) be a sequence of elements of M(x, y). There exist:
• A subsequence of (un)
• Critical points x0 = x, x1, . . . , xℓ, xℓ+1 = y
• Sequences (sk
n) (for 0 ≤k ≤ℓ)
• And elements uk ∈M(xk, xk+1)
such that for every k = 0, . . . , ℓ,
lim

on the space L(x, z), for which this space is compact.
Corollary 9.1.9. The space L(x, z) is compact.

9.2 Broken Trajectories, Gluing: Statements
In Section 9.1.c, we showed that a sequence of solutions of the Floer equation
connecting two critical points a and b could converge to a “broken trajectory”.
The space of broken trajectories appears as a “compactiﬁcation” of the space
of trajectories L(a, b). Let us study its structure in more detail when the
diﬀerence of the indices of a and b is 2.
Theorem 9.2.1. Let (H, J) be a regular pair (with H nondegenerate). Let x
and z be two periodic trajectories of H whose indices satisfy
µ(x) = µ(z) + 2.
Then L(x, z) is a compact manifold of dimension 1 with boundary and
∂L(x, y) =
S
µ(x)<µ(y)<µ(z)
L(x, y) × L(y, z).

Corollary 9.2.2. Under the same assumptions,

The remainder of this chapter is devoted to the proof of Theorem 9.2.1.
We have already shown, in Section 9.1.c, that the space of trajectories L(x, z)
is compact and that L(x, z) is a manifold of dimension 1. It therefore suﬃces
to study what happens in the neighborhood of the boundary points. This is
described in the following so-called “gluing” theorem.
Theorem 9.2.3. Let x, y and z be three critical points of the action func-
tional AH with consecutive indices
µ(x) = µ(y) + 1 = µ(z) + 2
and let (u, v) ∈M(x, y) × M(y, z) represent trajectories
(bu, bv) ∈L(x, y) × L(y, z).
We then have:
• There exists a diﬀerentiable map ψ : [ρ0, +∞[ →M(x, z) (for some ρ0 > 0)
such that π ◦ψ is an embedding
bψ = π ◦ψ : [ρ0, +∞[ −→L(x, z)
satisfying
lim
ρ→+∞
bψ(ρ) = (bu, bv) ∈L(x, z) ⊃L(x, z).
• Moreover, if ℓn ∈L(x, z) is a sequence that tends to (bu, bv), then ℓn ∈Im( bψ)
for n suﬃciently large.
Remark 9.2.4. The last assertion is as essential as its analogue in the Morse
case (see Remark 3.2.9).
The proof is considerably more diﬃcult than that of the corresponding
result in Morse theory, Proposition 3.2.8. We break it up into three steps:
(1) A “pre-gluing”, where we construct an interpolation wρ between the so-
lutions u and v of the Floer equation depending on a parameter ρ, which
will not exactly be a solution but will be an “approximated” solution in
the sense we will give later
(2) The construction of ψ, which we will write as
ψ(ρ) = expwρ(γ(ρ))

PDF PAGE 335
9.3 Pre-gluing
325
for some
γ(ρ) ∈W 1,p(w⋆
ρTW) = TwρP(x, z)
such that ψ(ρ) is a true solution of the Floer equation; we will obtain it
from wρ through a variant of Newton’s method, due to Picard, which we
will call the “Newton–Picard method”
(3) The veriﬁcation of the three desired properties of bψ = π ◦ψ, namely:
• limρ→+∞bψ(ρ) = (bu, bv).
•
bψ is an embedding.
•
bψ is unique in the sense of the last assertion of Theorem 9.2.3.
For the proofs we will need estimates of the derivatives of the exponential
map and of the Floer operator. Their proofs are not diﬃcult, but they are
very long (and some readers may ﬁnd them “morally obvious”). For this
reason, and to ease the reading, we will gather these in Chapter 13. We will
point out these technical lemmas as they come up in the text.

The Newton–Picard Method, General Idea.
We are seeking a γ = (γ1, . . . , γ2n) that is a solution of the equation
Fρ(γ) = 0. We just noted that even if we do not have Fρ(0) = 0, the ori-
gin 0 is “almost” a solution of the equation. We will use this approximate
solution as a starting point for the construction of a true solution using the
Newton–Picard method.
In the case of functions f : R →R, we know that this allows us to ﬁnd a
zero, α of f from an approximate solution x0 in the form of the limit of the
recursive sequence
xn+1 = xn −f(xn)
f ′(xn).
A variant is to ﬁnd α as the limit of the sequence
xn+1 = xn −f(xn)
f ′(x0),
that has the advantage of using only one value of the derivative and that we
will use here for Fρ. The two methods are represented in Figure 9.6.

The (Abstract) Newton–Picard Method.
Here is the precise statement we will use.
Lemma 9.4.4. Let X and Y be two Banach spaces and let F : X →Y be a
continuous map. We write
F(x) = F(0) + L(x) + N(x),
where L(x) = (dF)0(x) and we suppose that there exists a continuous G :
Y →X such that:
(1) L ◦G = Id.
(2) ∥GN(x) −GN(y)∥≤C(∥x∥+ ∥y∥) ∥x −y∥for all x, y ∈B(0, r).
(3) ∥GF(0)∥≤ε/2 where ε = min (r, 1/5C).
Then there exists a unique α ∈Im(G)∩B(0, ε) such that F(α) = 0. Moreover,
we have ∥α∥≤2 ∥GF(0)∥.
Proof. Keeping the classical Newton–Picard method in mind, we deﬁne ϕ :
B(0, ε) →Im(G) by setting
ϕ(x) = G(L(x) −F(x)) = −G(F(0) + N(x)).
Note that
Lϕ(x) = L(x) −F(x).

334
9 Spaces of Trajectories
Hence if ϕ(x) = x, then F(x) = 0. Conversely, if F(x) = 0 and x = G(y),
then
ϕ(x) = GL(x) = GLG(y) = G(y) = x.
We will therefore show that ϕ has a unique ﬁxed point in B(0, ε).
First, ϕ sends the ball with radius ε into itself: if ∥x∥≤ε, then
∥ϕ(x)∥= ∥G(F(0) + N(x))∥≤∥GF(0)∥+ ∥GN(x)∥
≤ε

well as the fact that N(0) = 0.

≤2Cε ∥x −y∥
≤2

therefore assert that

the technical lemmas proved in Chapter 13 (in Section 13.2):
∥(dN)z∥≤K ∥z∥
because

≤C−1K(∥x∥+ ∥y∥) ∥x −y∥
and condition (2) is satisﬁed.

Lemma 9.4.11. Let D : E →F be a (linear) Fredholm operator. Let (xn) be
a bounded sequence of elements of E such that the sequence (D(xn)) tends to 0
in F. Then there exists a subsequence of (xn) that converges to an element x
of Ker D ⊂E.
Proof. Since D is a Fredholm operator, we know that there exists an opera-
tor D′ such that
D′ ◦D = Id +K,
where K is an operator of ﬁnite rank (Proposition C.2.5), which implies that
the sequence (xn + K(xn)) tends to 0. After extracting a subsequence, if
necessary, the sequence (K(xn)) is convergent, giving a subsequence of (xn)
that converges.

This concludes the proof of Lemma 9.4.10.

9.5 Properties of b

We want to show that bψ is an immersion. To do this, we must verify that
∂ψ/∂ρ is not in the kernel of the projection dπ. Here π denotes taking the quo-
tient M(x, z) →L(x, z), so that the kernel of (dπ)ψ is generated by ∂ψ/∂s.
Supposing that ψ is not an immersion is therefore equivalent to supposing
the existence of sequences (ρn) and (αn) with

So bψ is an immersion (for ρ suﬃciently large). But it is also injective:

sion bψ is injective (by Rolle’s theorem).
9.6 Properties of b
ψ: Uniqueness of the Gluing
We have just shown that the broken trajectory (bu, bv) ∈L(x, y)×L(y, z) is the
endpoint of an interval embedded in L(x, z) whose interior is in a component
of L(x, z). This is not suﬃcient to conclude that L(x, z) is a manifold of
dimension 1 with boundary, as shown in Figure 9.9.
(u,v)
Fig. 9.9
We must still show that the only way to approximate (bu, bv) in L(x, z)
(other than with the constant sequence) is by passing through the interval
that we just constructed. This is the last assertion of Theorem 9.2.3 (which
paraphrases the corresponding statement for the Morse complex, that is,
Proposition 3.2.8): if a sequence in L(x, z) converges to (bu, bv), then it is in
the image of bψ.
The diﬃculty comes from the fact that the constructions of the gluing and
of the map bψ depend on a number of arbitrary choices. Consider, for example,
a function ν of class C∞, from R+ to R+ and with limρ→+∞ν(ρ) = +∞.
We can deﬁne a pre-gluing wν,ρ by replacing v(s −ρ) by v(s −ν(ρ)) in the
formulas that we used:

9.6 Properties of bψ: Uniqueness of the Gluing
349
The readers are invited to verify that by taking up the construction of the
gluing from wν,ρ, they obtain a continuous map
ψν : [ρν, +∞[ −→M(x, z)
such that
lim
ρ→+∞π ◦ψν(ρ) = (bu, bv).
They will then verify that if ν is increasing, then the map bψν = π ◦ψν is an
injective immersion. The case of bψ is the one where ν = Id.
In order to avoid the situation sketched in Figure 9.9, we need to ensure
that the image of the new map bψν is contained (for ρ suﬃciently large) in
that of the gluing bψ = bψId constructed before. This will be the ﬁrst step of
our proof, which will consist of four steps:
(1) The ﬁrst step, which we just described, consists in showing the following
result.
Proposition 9.6.1. Let ν : R+ →R+ be an increasing function of class
C∞such that limρ→+∞ν(ρ) = +∞. There exists a ρν > 0 such that for
ρ ≥ρν, we have bψν(ρ) ∈Im( bψ).
(2) For the second step, consider a sequence (ℓn)n∈N that tends to (bu, bv) in
L(x, z). As in Theorem 9.2.3, we want to prove that ℓn ∈Im( bψ) for n
suﬃciently large.
Remark 9.6.2. It suﬃces to prove that there exists a subsequence
(ℓnk)k∈N such that ℓnk ∈Im( bψ) for k suﬃciently large.
Indeed, suppose that the conclusion of Theorem 9.2.3 is false. We would
then have a subsequence ℓnk that is not in the image of bψ but tends to
(bu, bv) in L(x, z). In that case, we can extract a subsequence contained in
Im( bψ) from a certain rank on, giving a contradiction.
In the following statements, the conclusions concerning the sequence
(ℓn)n∈N hold after extracting a subsequence, if necessary. In this second
step we prove the following result.
Proposition 9.6.3. There exist a lift eℓn ∈M(x, z) of ℓn and an increasing
function ν : R+ →R+ of class C∞with limρ→+∞ν(ρ) = +∞such that
for every (s, t) ∈R × S1,
eℓn(s, t) = expwν,ρn(s,t) Yn(s, t),

9.6.b Step 2
We begin by establishing a number of preliminary results. Let us return to
the action functional AH : L(W) →R, whose critical points are the 1-
periodic orbits of the Hamiltonian vector ﬁeld Xt. We assume, as authorized
by Lemma 6.3.5, that AH takes on distinct values in distinct critical points.
Consider a sequence ℓn ∈L(x, z) with limn→+∞ℓn = (bu, bv), as in the state-
ment of Theorem 9.2.3. We have the following result.
Proposition 9.6.8. Let eℓn be a lift of ℓn in M(x, z). If the sequence eℓn con-
verges to a limit λ in M, then
λ ∈{x, eu, y, ev, z} ,
where eu and ev are lifts of bu and bv, respectively, in M(x, z).
To prove this proposition, we will use the following lemma.
Lemma 9.6.9. Let sn be a real sequence that has a limit and let λn be a
sequence in M. We assume that, in the C∞
loc sense,
lim

and

for two trajectories a ∈M(α, β), b ∈M(γ, δ) (α, β, γ, δ are critical points
of AH). If ba ̸= bb, then the sequence sn is divergent and we have
AH(α) ≥AH(β) ≥AH(γ) ≥AH(δ)
if lim sn = +∞,

if lim sn = −∞.

Proof of Lemma 9.6.9. If sn has a ﬁnite limit σ, then we obviously have
a(s + σ, t) = b(s, t), hence ba = bb. We therefore assume that lim sn = +∞
and ﬁx σ, τ ∈R. For n suﬃciently large, we have sn + σ ≥τ. Since AH is
decreasing on the trajectories of M, we therefore have
AH(λn(sn + σ, ·) ≤AH(τ, ·)).
By letting n tend to inﬁnity, we obtain
AH(b(σ, ·)) ≤AH(a(τ, ·))
for all σ and τ in R. By letting σ tend to −∞and τ to +∞, we ﬁnd that
AH(β) ≥AH(γ),
which proves the lemma (the case of a sequence sn that tends to −∞is
analogous).

Proof of Proposition 9.6.8. We now apply this lemma to the sequence eℓn.

In particular, AH(λ(s, ·)) does not depend on s, hence λ is a periodic orbit
of Xt. Since we have assumed that the critical values of AH are distinct
(Lemma 6.3.5), we have now shown that
λ ∈{x, y, z} .

9.6.d Step 4 (Last Step)
We begin with a more general version of the Newton–Picard method, a gen-
eralization of Lemma 9.4.4, whose notation we keep.
Lemma 9.6.16. Let X and Y be two Banach spaces and let F : X →Y be
a continuous map. We write
F(x) = F(0) + L(x) + N(x),
where L(x) = (dF)0(x), and we suppose that there exists a continuous
G : Y →X such that:
(1) L ◦G = Id.
(2) ∥GN(x) −GN(y)∥≤C(∥x∥+ ∥y∥) ∥x −y∥for all x, y ∈B(0, r).
(3) ∥GF(0)∥≤ε0/2 where ε0 = min (r, 1/5C).
Let h ∈Ker L satisfy ∥h∥< ε0/5. Then there exists a unique γh ∈
(h + Im G) ∩B(0, ε0) such that F(γh) = 0. Moreover,
∥γh∥≤∥GF(0)∥+ ∥h∥.
Proof. It is similar to that of the original, Lemma 9.4.4. We consider the map
ϕ : B(0, ε0) →Im G deﬁned by
ϕ(x) = G(L(x) −F(x)) = −G(F(0) + N(x)),
which therefore satisﬁes L(ϕ(x)) = L(x)−F(x). We then deﬁne the sequence
x0 = h,
xn+1 = ϕ(xn) + h.
As in the proof of Lemma 9.4.4, for x ∈B(0, ε0), we have
∥ϕ(x) + h∥≤∥ϕ(x)∥+ ∥h∥≤ε0
2 + ε0
5 + ε0
5 < ε0,

PDF PAGE 378
368
9 Spaces of Trajectories
which shows that xn ∈B(0, ε0) for every n. Still following the proof of
Lemma 9.4.4, we then have
∥xn+1 −xn∥= ∥ϕ(xn) −ϕ(xn−1)∥≤1
2 ∥xn −xn−1∥
(hence ϕ is a contracting map), whence
lim xn = γh ∈B(0, ε0) ∩{h + Im G}
and ϕ(γh) + h = γh. We therefore have
L(γh) = L(ϕ(γh) + h) = Lϕ(γh) = L(γh) −F(γh),
which implies that F(γh) = 0.
Let us show the uniqueness: if γ ∈h + Im G ∩B(0, ε0) satisﬁes F(γ) = 0,
then we set γ = h + G(y) and obtain
ϕ(γ) = GL(γ) = GL(h + G(y)) = GLG(y) = G(y) = γ −h,
hence γ = ϕ(γ) + h. But this equation admits a unique solution because ϕ is
a contraction, as we just saw.

Chapter 10
From Floer To Morse
In this chapter, we prove that in the case of a nondegenerate Hamiltonian
that does not depend on time and is suﬃciently small (in the sense of the
C2 topology), when we are able to deﬁne the Morse complex and the Floer
complex; the two coincide.
10.1 The Results
For the sake of simplicity and to alleviate the writing, we have used more or
less the same notation for the Morse complex (in Chapter 3) and for the Floer
complex (in Chapter 9). At this point, we need to distinguish between the two
(in order to show that they coincide). From now on, let CF⋆(H, J) denote the
Floer complex associated with the Hamiltonian H and the almost complex
structure J (which was called (C⋆, ∂) in Chapter 9), and let CM⋆(H, J)
denote the Morse complex associated with the Morse function H and the
vector ﬁeld grad H, the gradient of H for the metric deﬁned by J (and ω)
(which was called (C⋆(H), ∂gradH) in Chapter 3).
We want to prove the following.
Theorem 10.1.1. There exists a nondegenerate, suﬃciently small (for the
C2 topology) Hamiltonian H for which
CF⋆(H, J) = CM⋆+n(H, J).
This statement of course also contains the fact that the two complexes in
question are well deﬁned. Starting with a nondegenerate Hamiltonian H0, we
construct H as H = H0/k for a suﬃciently large k.
373

PDF PAGE 384
374
10 From Floer To Morse
Since H is suﬃciently small, its periodic trajectories are constant (Proposi-
tion 6.1.5), hence Crit(AH) = Crit(H). On the other hand, Proposition 5.4.5,
which compares the two notions of nondegeneracy (of the critical points of
the function H and of the periodic trajectories of the Hamiltonian H), implies
that H is a Morse function and that Crit(AH) = Crit(H).
By Remark 5.4.6, we know that the Hessian of H has no eigenvalues in 2πZ,
so that Proposition 7.2.1 allows us to compare the (Morse) index of a critical
point of H to the (Maslov) index of the corresponding constant trajectory:
IndH(x) = µ(x) + n.
This implies that the vector spaces that occur in the deﬁnitions of the two
complexes are the same up to a shift in the indices.
Next, we need to consider the two diﬀerentials. More exactly, we must:
• Be able to deﬁne the diﬀerentials of the two complexes
• Show that they coincide.
To deﬁne the diﬀerential of the Morse complex, we need a vector ﬁeld X
adapted to the Morse function H, which satisﬁes the Smale condition. On the
other hand, we want a relation between the trajectories of this vector ﬁeld
and the solutions of the Floer equation, that is, between the solutions of
du
ds + X(u) = 0
and
∂u
∂s + J(u)∂u
∂t + grad H(u) = 0.
We therefore want X to be the gradient of the function H for the metric
deﬁned by an almost complex structure J calibrated by ω. We will therefore
prove the following result.
Theorem 10.1.2. Let H be a Morse function on the symplectic manifold
(W, ω). There exists a dense subset Jreg(H) consisting of almost complex
structures J calibrated by ω such that the pair (H, −JXH) is Morse-Smale.
We will carry out the proof in two steps. The ﬁrst will be devoted to a
general Morse function f and an adapted vector ﬁeld X. We will linearize
the equation
du
ds + X(u) = 0
of the ﬂow of the vector ﬁeld −X along one of its solutions in LuY = 0, and
then show the following result.
Theorem 10.1.3. When f is a Morse function and u is a trajectory con-
necting two critical points x and y, Lu is a Fredholm operator whose index is
the diﬀerence of the Morse indices of the two critical points.

PDF PAGE 385
10.1 The Results
375
Because of the relation between the Morse and Maslov indices mentioned
earlier, this immediately implies the following corollary.
Corollary 10.1.4. For a nondegenerate Hamiltonian H and a trajectory u
of −JXH, the Fredholm operators (dF)u and Lu have the same index.
We will also show the following result.
Theorem 10.1.5. The vector ﬁeld X is Smale if and only if the operators Lu
are surjective.
In the second step, we will consider the speciﬁc case of the Hamiltonian H
and prove Theorem 10.1.2, a transversality result analogous to the ones we
established in Section 8.5.
Having chosen the almost complex structure and therefore the Smale vec-
tor ﬁeld, we will then compare the solutions of the Floer equation and the
trajectories of the vector ﬁeld.
Remark 10.1.6. It is clear that the solutions of the Floer equation that do
not depend on t are exactly the trajectories of the vector ﬁeld X = −grad H.
We want to prove the following proposition.
Proposition 10.1.7. If H is suﬃciently small (in the C2 sense), then
Ker(dF)u = Ker Lu.
We will prove this later. The elements of the kernel of (dF)u therefore do
not depend on t. Using the identity of the indices (Corollary 10.1.4 and the
characterization of the Smale vector ﬁelds, in Theorem 10.1.5), this implies
the following result.
Corollary 10.1.8. The Fredholm operator (dF)u is surjective along every
trajectory of the gradient of H.
On the other hand, we will show that, after replacing H by Hk = H/k
(which has the same critical points with the same indices) for k suﬃciently
large, if necessary, all solutions of the Floer equation that interest us are
independent of t.
Proposition 10.1.9. If k is suﬃciently large, then the solutions of the Floer
equation for Hk connecting critical points x and y with
IndHk(x) −IndHk(y) ≤2
are all independent of t.

PDF PAGE 386
376
10 From Floer To Morse
Let Hk = H/k with k suﬃciently large, and let J ∈Jreg (the dense subset
given by Theorem 10.1.2). The conclusion is that when Ind(x) −Ind(y) ≤2,
the trajectories of the Floer equation associated with (H, J) that connect the
two critical points x and y (critical points both for H and for AH since the
two sets of critical points coincide) are exactly the trajectories of the Smale
vector ﬁeld X = −JXH (by Proposition 10.1.9). Moreover, the Floer operator
linearized along these trajectories is surjective (by Corollary 10.1.8). Such a
regularity condition implies that M(H,J)(x, y) is a manifold, which allows us
to deﬁne the Floer complex (see Theorem 8.1.2).
We establish this regularity property only for Ind(x)−Ind(y)≤2 (and not
in general, as in Chapter 8 (see Theorem 8.1.1)), but this case suﬃces to
construct the Floer complex CF⋆(H, J). We have seen that it coincides with
the Morse complex CM⋆+n(H, J), which proves Theorem 10.1.1. We still
need to show the intermediate results (Theorems10.1.2, 10.1.3, 10.1.5 and
Proposition 10.1.7).

10.2.b The Exponential Decay of the Solutions
First note that if Y is a solution of LuY = 0 in W 1,2(R; Rn), then it is
continuous, hence since it satisﬁes the diﬀerential equation, it is of class C1,
and so on, so that the solutions in W 1,2 are C∞(elementary bootstrapping).
In particular, the solutions form a ﬁnite-dimensional vector space.
Let us study the behavior at inﬁnity that the inclusion in W 1,2 imposes on
the solutions. In the neighborhood of a critical point, say x, we use a Morse
chart of V . We can choose the trivialization of TV in a standard way in this
chart, and the diﬀerential equation LuY = 0 can simply be written as
dY
ds = −AY,
where A is a constant matrix that we may, and do, assume to be diagonal,
so that the system is
dyi
ds = −λiyi,
1 ≤i ≤n
and its solutions are of the form
yi(s) = yie−λis.
It is clear that for such a vector to be in W 1,2(R; Rn), it must tend to 0
when s →−∞, which it can only do exponentially (and likewise at +∞).
Let us give an interpretation in terms of the operator Lu of the properties
of the function f and the vector ﬁeld X that we have used to construct the
Morse complex.
10.2.c The Fredholm Property
More precisely, we will prove the following.
Proposition 10.2.2. If u is a trajectory of the gradient connecting two non-
degenerate critical points x and y, then the operator Lu is a Fredholm oper-
ator.

Corollary 10.2.7. If f is a Morse function, then for every trajectory u, the
operator Lu is a Fredholm operator.
⊓⊔
10.2.d Computation of the Index
Let us now compute the index of this operator.
Proposition 10.2.8. The index of the Fredholm operator Lu is
Ind(x) −Ind(y).
Proof. We have already noted that the solutions of LuY = 0 in W 1,2 tend
to 0 exponentially when s →±∞. If σ and s are two real numbers, then let
Φ(σ,s) : Tu(σ)V −→Tu(s)V

at σ. Let

10.2.e The Smale Condition
We will now prove that the vector ﬁeld X satisﬁes the Smale condition if and
only if all Lu are surjective, in other words, Theorem 10.1.5.
Remark 10.2.9. If X is the gradient of f for a metric g, then this condition
of course concerns that metric.
Proof of Theorem 10.1.5. This is a consequence of the computation given
above. Saying that Lu is surjective is saying that its cokernel is reduced to 0,
in other words, that L⋆
u is injective, or that
Tu(σ)W u(x) + Tu(σ)W s(y) = Tu(σ)V.
The latter is the transversality condition of the stable and unstable manifolds,
which we want for all critical points x and y.
⊓⊔
We could also have worked out the theory of the Morse complex in this
setting (see [65, 68]).

10.3 Proof of Theorem 10.1.2 (Regularity)
The Hamiltonian H is now ﬁxed and we consider two critical points x and y.
Let Z(x, y, H) be deﬁned as
{(u, J) | J ∈Jc(ω) and u is a trajectory of −JXH connecting x to y} .
10.3.a Inﬁnitesimal Variations of the Almost Complex Structure
The space Jc(ω) of almost complex structures calibrated by ω is a subset of
the vector space of sections of the ﬁber bundle of endomorphisms of TW. We
have already noted (Proposition 5.5.7) that the tangent space is
TJJc(ω) = {S ∈End(TW) | JS + SJ = 0 and ω(Sξ, η) + ω(ξ, Sη) = 0} .
We ﬁx such an S and set Jt = J exp(−tJS).
Lemma 10.3.1. For t suﬃciently small, the endomorphism Jt is an almost
complex structure calibrated by ω.
Proof. First of all, Jt is an almost complex structure: since J is “anticommu-
tative” with S, Jt = exp(tJS)J and therefore
J2
t = exp(tJS)JJ exp(−tJS) = J2 = −Id .
Next, Jt is an isometry of ω because
d

dtω(exp(−tJS)ξ, exp(−tJS)η)

= ω(SJ exp(−tJS)ξ, exp(−tJS)η)
+ ω(exp(−tJS)ξ, SJ exp(−tJS)η)
= ω(SJΞ, H) + ω(Ξ, SJH) = 0
(where we have set Ξ = exp(−tJS)ξ and H = exp(−tJS)η (uppercase η)),
hence ω(Jtξ, Jtη) is constant and equal to ω(Jξ, Jη) = ω(ξ, η). This compu-
tation also gives
ω(Jtξ, η) = ω(J2
t ξ, Jtη) = −ω(ξ, Jtη) = ω(Jtη, ξ),
so that ω(·, Jt·) is a symmetric bilinear form. Next, we have
ω(ξ, Jtη) = ω(ξ, Jη) + O(t),

10.4.b The Trajectories Are Independent of t
We will now show that the trajectories of H/k that count for the Floer
complex are exactly those that count for the Morse complex, in other words,
Proposition 10.1.9.
Proof of Proposition 10.1.9. We assume that it is not true. This means that
there exist a sequence (nk) that tends to inﬁnity and a solution unk (that
eﬀectively depends on t) that we can assume to connect the critical points x
and y, of the Floer equation
∂u
∂s + J ∂u
∂t + 1
nk
grad H = 0.
Let
vnk(s, t) = unk(nks, nkt).
This is a solution of the Floer equation for the original Hamiltonian H, which
is periodic of period 1/nk. We may therefore consider it of period 1, so that
vnk ∈M(x, y, H).

PDF PAGE 403
10.4 The Morse and Floer Trajectories Coincide
393
First consider the case where the diﬀerence of the indices is 1;
Ind(x) −Ind(y) = 1 = µ(x) −µ(y).
By the convergence theorem toward broken trajectories (Theorem 9.1.7), af-
ter extracting a subsequence, if necessary, we may, and do, assume that the
sequence (vnk) tends to a limit v ∈M(x, y, H). If we show that v does not
depend on t, then since (dF)v is surjective (by Corollary 10.1.8), v must be
in a component of M(x, y, H) of dimension 1, that is, must be an isolated
point of L(x, y). For k suﬃciently large, we would then have
vnk(s, t) = v(s + σk, t) = v(s + σk),
and vnk would not depend on t, a contradiction with the assumption we
made.
It therefore suﬃces to prove that the limit v does not depend on t. We use
the fact that v is the limit of a sequence whose period tends to 0. Let r ∈R.
We ﬁx an s ∈R and a t ∈[0, 1]. Since vnk is periodic of period 1/nk, we have
vnk(s, t) = vnk

s, t + [rnk]
nk

(where [rnk] denotes the integral part of rnk). When k tends to inﬁnity,
[rnk]/nk tends to r, so that the left-hand side tends to v(s, t) while the right-
hand side tends to v(s, t + r). Consequently, for every r,
v(s, t) = v(s, t + r),
which we wanted to prove.
We now assume that the diﬀerence of the indices is 2;
Ind(x) −Ind(y) = 2 = µ(x) −µ(y).
In this case, Theorem 9.1.7 states that a subsequence of (vnk(s+sk, t)) tends
either to a limit v ∈M(x, y, H) or to a broken trajectory
(v, w) ∈M(x, z) × M(z, y).
In the ﬁrst case, we prove as above that v is independent of t. Once again,
(dF)v is surjective by Corollary 10.1.8. As in the proof of Lemma 8.5.9, we
therefore ﬁnd that M(x, y, H) is a submanifold of Z(x, y, J) of dimension 2

PDF PAGE 404
394
10 From Floer To Morse
in a neighborhood of (v, H) (we use the notation
Z(x, y, J) = {(v, H + h) | h ∈C∞
ε (H), v ∈M(x, y, J, H + h)}
of Chapter 8). Moreover, since the gradient −JXH of H has the Smale prop-
erty, its trajectories that connect x to y form a manifold of dimension 2. But
these trajectories are also in M(x, y, H), so that we obtain a parametrization
of this submanifold in the neighborhood of v, by trajectories that do not
depend on t. Next, since vnk(s + sk, t) ∈M(x, y, H), and since this sequence
tends to v, it follows that vnk(s + sk, t) is independent of t for k suﬃciently
large, a contradiction.
We are left with the case where lim vnk = (v, w), that is, where there exist

the two operators (dF)v and (dF)w are surjective. We can then apply the

On the other hand, v and w are also (Morse) trajectories of the Smale ﬁeld
−JXH. In this setting, the gluing theorem (that is, Theorem 3.2.7) states that
the broken trajectory (bu, bv) is a boundary point of a manifold of dimension 1
consisting of Morse trajectories that connect x to y. But these are also Floer
trajectories, so that we have a map
ϕ : [ρ0, +∞[ −→M(x, y, H)
such that
bϕ : [ρ0, +∞[ −→L(x, y)
is an embedding. In addition, when ρ tends to +∞, bϕ(ρ) tends to (bv, bw) in
the sense of the convergence toward broken orbits in Morse theory (which is
deﬁned in Section 3.2.a). In particular, this means that

is deﬁned by Theorem 9.1.7).
In summary, we have three convergences toward (bv, bw) (see Figure 10.1):
• The convergence of (vnk) we assumed for k tending to inﬁnity
• The convergence of bψ(ρ) for ρ →+∞given by the “Floer” gluing
• The convergence of bϕ(ρ) for ρ →+∞given by the “Morse” gluing.
ϕ(ρ)
(v, w)
vnk
ψ(ρ)
Fig. 10.1
We now apply the “uniqueness” part of the gluing theorem (Theorem 9.2.3).
It implies that for k suﬃciently large, (vnk) is in the image of bψ. The same
uniqueness argument also implies that for ρ suﬃciently large, say ρ ≥ρ1,
bϕ(ρ) ∈Im bψ. Moreover, bϕ([ρ1, +∞[) is an interval in the manifold L(x, y),
which is of dimension 1. Since
lim
ρ→+∞bϕ(ρ) =
lim
ρ→+∞
bψ(ρ) = (bv, bw) /∈L(x, y),

PDF PAGE 406
396
10 From Floer To Morse
this means that bψ(ρ) ∈Im bϕ for ρ suﬃciently large. In particular, bvnk ∈Im bϕ
for k suﬃciently large, which contradicts the fact that vnk does not depend
on t.
⊓⊔
We have now shown that all Floer solutions connecting two consecutive
critical points are Morse trajectories and that (dF)v is surjective along these
trajectories. We therefore have the desired equality of the complexes.

PDF PAGE 407
Chapter 11
Floer Homology: Invariance
We have just deﬁned the Floer homology HF⋆(H, J) as the homology of the
complex CF⋆(H, J). The object of this chapter is to show that the homology
HF⋆(H, J) does not depend on the chosen regular pair (H, J) ∈(H × J)reg
(in the sense of Section 8.1).
To prove this we will use a method analogous to the one that allowed us
to show the invariance of Morse homology in Section 3.4. We will proceed
as follows: let (Ha, Ja) and (Hb, Jb) be two pairs in (H × J)reg. We consider
a smooth homotopy Γ = (H, J) that connects (Ha, Ja) to (Hb, Jb); more
precisely,
H : R × S1 × W −→R
and
J : R −→End(TW)
with J(s) ∈J for every s and
(
H(s, ·, ·) = Ha
if s ≤−R
H(s, ·, ·) = Hb
if s ≥R,
and
(
J(s) = Ja
if s ≤−R
J(s) = Jb
if s ≥R.
Here R is a constant (that will not necessarily be the same for all homotopies).
The existence of such homotopies follows from the fact that J is contractible
(Proposition 5.5.6).
The proof is carried out in two steps:
(1) Starting from a homotopy Γ, we deﬁne (in Sections 11.1 and 11.2) a mor-
phism of complexes
ΦΓ : CF⋆(Ha, Ja) −→CF⋆(Hb, Jb)
and we verify that for the stationary homotopy Γ connecting (Ha, Ja) to
itself, this morphism ΦΓ is the identity (Proposition 11.1.14).
397

PDF PAGE 408
398
11 Floer Homology: Invariance
(2) If (Ha, Ja), (Hb, Jb) and (Hc, Jc) are pairs in (H × J)reg and if Γ, Γ ′
and Γ ′′ are homotopies connecting (Ha, Ja) to (Hc, Jc), (Ha, Ja) to
(Hb, Jb) and (Hb, Jb) to (Hc, Jc), respectively, then we prove that the
morphisms
ΦΓ ′′ ◦ΦΓ ′
and
ΦΓ : CF⋆(Ha, Ja) −→CF⋆(Hc, Jc)
are homotopic and in particular induce the same morphism at the homol-
ogy level. This second step in turn has two substeps:
(a) We ﬁrst prove that two homotopies Γ1 and Γ2 deﬁne homotopic mor-
phisms ΦΓ1 and ΦΓ2 (Proposition 11.2.8, proved in Sections 11.3 and
11.4).
(b) We then prove the existence of a homotopy Γ for which the morphisms
of complexes ΦΓ ′′ ◦ΦΓ ′ and ΦΓ coincide (Proposition 11.2.9, proved in
Section 11.5).
As in Section 3.4, we then deduce the invariance of the Floer homology by
considering the special case where (Ha, Ja) = (Hc, Jc) and Γ = Id. It is clear
that ΦΓ ′ and ΦΓ ′′ then induce isomorphisms that are each other’s inverses
at the homology level.

11.1.b The Transversality
We return to the results of Chapter 8 and adapt them to the Floer equation
with parameters. The dependence on s will simplify some of the proofs, as
we will see: a larger source space will make the surjectivity easier to show. As
before, let Γ = (H, J) be a homotopy that connects (Ha, Ja) and (Hb, Jb).

(in s), the Floer trajectories of the perturbed homotopy near the critical

Theorem 11.1.6. There exist a neighborhood of 0 in C∞

For the sake of simplicity, we left out in the notation the fact that the

Proof of Theorem 11.1.10. It begins with a lemma.

xk = x′). The theorem has now been now proved.

Theorem 11.1.15. For x ∈Crit AHa and z ∈Crit AHb with µ(x)−µ(z) = 1,

convergence). As in Proposition 9.1.2, this topology is Hausdorﬀand The-

11.1.d The Gluing
For µ(x) −µ(z) = 1, the elements of ΠΓ (x, z) are broken trajectories (u, v).
One of these trajectories is a solution of the Floer equation with parameters
and the other is a solution of the Floer equation without parameters associ-
ated with (Ha, Ja) or (Hb, Jb). To prove Theorem 11.1.15, it will suﬃce to
glue the trajectories u and v as in Section 9.2. We will prove the following
theorem, which is analogous to the gluing theorem 9.2.3.
Theorem 11.1.16. Let x be a critical point of AHa and let y, z be critical
points of AHb such that
µ(x) = µ(y) = µ(z) + 1.
Let u ∈MΓ (x, y) and let bv ∈L(Hb,Jb)(y, z). Then:
• There exists an embedding
ψ : [ρ0, +∞[ −→MΓ (x, z)
(for some ρ0 > 0) such that
lim
ρ→+∞ψ(ρ) = (u, bv).
• Moreover, if (ℓn) is a sequence of elements of MΓ (x, z) that tends to (u, bv),
then ℓn ∈Im ψ for n suﬃciently large.
Remarks.
• The indices µ(y) and µ(z) correspond to Hb while µ(x) corresponds to
Ha, a dependence that we have left out of the notation.
• The convergence of ψ(ρ) toward (u, bv) asserted in this statement should
be viewed in the sense of Theorem 11.1.10.
• To prove Theorem 11.1.15, we also need to glue trajectories
bu′ ∈L(Ha,Ja)(x, y′)
and
v′ ∈MΓ (y′, z)
(for µ(y′) = µ(z) = µ(x)−1). Such a gluing is done in a manner analogous
to that described by Theorem 11.1.16 and we can thus show that in the

PDF PAGE 422
412
11 Floer Homology: Invariance
neighborhood of the points of ΠΓ (x, z), the space MΓ (x, z) ∪ΠΓ (x, z) is
a manifold of dimension 1. This concludes the proof of Theorem 11.1.15.
We will devote the next section to the proof of Theorem 11.1.16.
11.2 Proof of Theorem 11.1.16

(3) Finally, we ﬁnish the proof.
Proof of Proposition 11.2.3. Recall that R denotes a real positive number
such that for s ≥R, the homotopy Γ(s) is stationary, equal to (Hb, Jb). To
simplify the notation, we will, in this proof, leave out the variable t ∈S1 in
the expressions of the maps deﬁned on R × S1.
We begin the proof with an analogue of Lemma 9.6.9.
Lemma 11.2.5. Let (λn) be a sequence in MΓ (x, z) and let (sn), (σn) be
two real sequences tending to +∞. Suppose that
lim
n→+∞λn(s + sn) = a(s)
and
lim
n→+∞λn(s + σn) = b(s)
for
a ∈M(Hb,Jb)(α, β),
b ∈M(Hb,Jb)(γ, δ)
(α, β, γ and δ are critical points of AHb). Then we have:
• If limn→+∞(sn −σn) = −∞, then
AHb(α) ≥AHb(β) ≥AHb(γ) ≥AHb(δ).
• If limn→+∞(sn −σn) = +∞, then
AHb(γ) ≥AHb(δ) ≥AHb(α) ≥AHb(β).
Proof. The two assertions can be proved in completely analogous manners.
Let us therefore show only the second one. We assume that
lim(sn −σn) = +∞.
For s ≥R, λn(s) is a solution of the Floer equation for (Hb, Jb), so that,
in particular, s 7→AHb(ℓn(s)) is a decreasing function on [R, +∞[. We ﬁx σ
and τ ∈R and choose n suﬃciently large to have
sn + τ > σn + σ ≥R.
We then have
AHb(λn(sn + τ)) < AHb(λn(σn + τ)).
Letting n tend to inﬁnity, we obtain
AHb(a(τ)) ≤AHb(b(σ)),
an inequality that holds for every τ and every σ. It suﬃces to let σ tend to
+∞and τ to −∞to conclude the proof of this lemma.
⊓⊔

420
11 Floer Homology: Invariance
We can use this lemma to prove the following.
Lemma 11.2.6. Let (ℓn) be a sequence in MΓ (x, z) that tends to (u, bv) ∈
MΓ (x, y) × L(Hb,Jb)(y, z). Then:
(1) There exists a real sequence (sn) with sn ≥R tending to +∞, such that
AHb(ℓn(sn)) = AHb(y).
(2) Moreover, limn→+∞ℓn(s + sn) = y.
Proof. As in the proof of the previous lemma, the function s 7→AHb(ℓn(s)) is
decreasing for s ≥R. Since u ∈MΓ (x, y), the same holds for s 7→AHb(u(s)).
We ﬁx an s ≥R. Since lim ℓn(s) = u(s), we have
lim
n→+∞AHb(ℓn(s)) = AHb(u(s)) > AHb(y).
In particular, AHb(ℓn(s)) > AHb(y) for n suﬃciently large.
We have ﬁxed a lift v ∈M(Hb,Jb)(y, z) of bv. The convergence of (ℓn) implies
the existence of a sequence (σn) that tends to +∞and satisﬁes
lim
n→+∞ℓn(s + σn) = v(s).
In particular, for the s ﬁxed above, we have
lim
n→+∞AHb(ℓn(s + σn)) = AHb(v(s)) < AHb(y),
which implies that
AHb(ℓn(s + σn)) < AHb(y)
for n suﬃciently large. From this we deduce the existence, for n suﬃciently
large, of an sn ∈]s, s + σn[ with AHb(ℓn(sn)) = AHb(y).
Suppose that the sequence (sn) is bounded. After extracting a subse-
quence, if necessary, we may, and do, assume that it tends to a limit s⋆≥R.
Then
lim
n→+∞ℓn(s + sn) = u(s + s⋆)
in C∞

In particular, lim ℓn(sn) = u(s⋆), hence
lim
n→+∞AHb(ℓn(sn)) = AHb(u(s⋆)) > AHb(y),
which gives a contradiction because the sequence on the left-hand side is
constant and equal to AHb(y). Consequently (always after extracting a sub-
sequence, if necessary), lim sn = +∞.

therefore have a = y, concluding the proof of the lemma.

Consequently, Theorem 11.1.15 is also completely proved. With this proof,

ΦΓ : CF⋆(Ha, Ja) −→CF⋆(Hb, Jb).
The aim of the second and last step of this outline is to prove that this
morphism induces an isomorphism at the homology level. This is in turn
divided into two steps corresponding to the following two statements.
Proposition 11.2.8. At the homology level, the morphism ΦΓ induces a
morphism that is independent of the choice of the regular homotopy Γ be-
tween (Ha, Ja) and (Hb, Jb).
Proposition 11.2.9. Let (Ha, Ja), (Hb, Jb) and (Hc, Jc) ∈(H × J)reg and
let Γ ′ and Γ ′′ be two regular homotopies connecting (Ha, Ja) to (Hb, Jb)
and (Hb, Jb) to (Hc, Jc), respectively. There exists a regular homotopy Γ
connecting (Ha, Ja) to (Hc, Jc) such that
ΦΓ ′′ ◦ΦΓ ′
and
ΦΓ : CF⋆(Ha, Ja) −→CF⋆(Hc, Jc)
induce the same homomorphism at the homology level.
These two propositions imply the relation
ΦΓ ′′ ◦ΦΓ ′ = ΦΓ at the homology level,
regardless of the choices of the regular homotopies Γ ′′, Γ ′ and Γ, as above.
This is step 2 of the outline of this chapter, as stated at its beginning. From

11.3 Invariance of ΦΓ : Proof of Proposition 11.2.8
Consider two regular homotopies Γ0 and Γ1 connecting (Ha, Ja) to (Hb, Jb).

To determine its dimension, we use the following lemma.
Lemma 11.3.6. Let h0 ∈Hreg and let (λ, u) ∈MΓ (h0)(x, y). The operator
Y : R × W 1,p(u⋆TW) −→Lp(u⋆TW)
deﬁned by Y(a, Y ) = aV + (dFΓλ(H+h0))u(Y ) is surjective.
In this statement, V still denotes the vector ﬁeld deﬁned and used in the
proof of Proposition 11.3.5.

PDF PAGE 444
434
11 Floer Homology: Invariance
Proof. First note that Ker Y
=
Ker(dπ)(λ,u,H+h0) is ﬁnite-dimensional
and that Im Y
=
RV + Im(dFΓλ)u is closed and ﬁnite-codimensional
in Lp(u⋆TW). In other words, Y is a Fredholm operator. Suppose that it
is not surjective. Then, as in Lemma 8.5.1, there exists a nonzero vector
ﬁeld Z ∈Lq(u⋆TW) (here 1/p + 1/q = 1) such that ⟨Z, Im Y⟩= 0, which is
equivalent to
⟨Z, V ⟩= 0
and
⟨Z, Im(dFΓλ)u⟩= 0.
The second condition implies that Z is of class C∞, by elliptic regularity, as
in the proof of Lemma 8.5.1. We now use the assumption that h0 ∈Hreg. It
implies that (dπ)(λ,u,H+h0) is surjective, which means that for every h ∈C∞
ε ,
there exists an (a, Y ) ∈R × W 1,p(u⋆TW) such that
aV + (dFΓλ)u(Y ) + gradu h = 0
(in other words, (a, Y, h) ∈T(λ,u,H+h0)Z(x, y, J)). It follows that
⟨Z, gradu h⟩= 0
for every h ∈C∞
ε ,
which gives a contradiction because of Lemma 11.1.9. This concludes the
proof of the lemma.
⊓⊔
With the notation of the previous lemma, we then ﬁnd that
dim MΓ (h)(x, y) = dim Ker(dπ)(λ,u,H+h0) = dim Ker Y = Ind Y.
To compute the Fredholm index of Y, we consider the path of opera-
tors (Yσ)σ∈[0,1]:
Yσ : R × W 1,p(u⋆TW) −→Lp(u⋆TW)
(a, Y ) 7−→aσV + (dFΓλ(H+h))u(Y ).
These are Fredholm operators because they are of the same form as Y. In
particular, their index does not depend on σ (by C.2.11), it is therefore equal
to that of Y0, but
Y0(a, Y ) = (dFΓλ)u(Y ),
hence
Ind Y = Ind Y0 = Ind(dFΓλ)u + 1 = µ(x) −µ(y) + 1
by Theorem 11.3.3. This concludes the proof of Theorem 11.3.2.
⊓⊔
From now on, we will assume that the homotopies Γ = (H, J) are reg-

µ(x) −µ(y) + 1, as in the conclusion of Theorem 11.3.2. We will moreover

To conclude the proof of Theorem 11.3.11 (ii), we use a gluing process
analogous to that of Section 11.1.d to construct, for every point of ΠΓ (x, z),

ment is as follows.
Theorem 11.3.14. Let x be a critical point of AHa, and let y, z be critical
points of AHb such that
µ(x) = µ(z) = µ(y) −1.

• There exists an embedding ψ : [ρ0, +∞[ →MΓ (x, z) (for some ρ0) such
that limρ→+∞ψ(ρ) = [(λ⋆, u), bv].
• If [λn, ℓn] is a sequence of elements of MΓ (x, z) that tends to [(λ⋆, u), bv],
then [λn, ℓn] ∈Im ψ for n suﬃciently large.
The convergence in this theorem is the one deﬁned in Theorem 11.3.10.
The proof is given in the following section.

Proposition 11.5.3. Let x ∈Crit(AHa) and z ∈Crit(AHc) be such that

We now prove this proposition. Here are the ideas of the proof. It comes
down to (which is not surprising):

PDF PAGE 466

11 Floer Homology: Invariance
(1) Deﬁning, for ρ suﬃciently large, a map
χρ :
S
y∈Crit(AHb)
µ(y)=µ(x)
MΓ ′(x, y) × MΓ ′′(y, z) −→MΓρ(x, z)
(2) Showing that it is injective for ρ suﬃciently large
(3) Showing that it is surjective for ρ suﬃciently large.
To deﬁne χρ(u, v) for a pair (u, v) ∈MΓ ′(x, y) × MΓ ′′(y, z), we use a gluing
process, as in Sections 9.4 and 11.3.d: from the pre-gluing wρ = u#ρv, we
construct a solution ϕ(ρ) ∈MΓρ using the Newton–Picard method.
As for the other Floer solutions obtained using this method, we will ﬁnd
that ϕρ tends to (u, v) (in the sense of the convergence toward broken orbits)
when ρ tends to inﬁnity; see Subsection 11.1.c.
To prove the injectivity of ϕ, we use a contradiction. If χρ were not injective
from some value of ρ, then there would exist a sequence ρn tending to inﬁnity
and pairs (un, vn) ̸= (u′
n, v′
n) such that χρn(un, vn) = χρn(u′
n, v′
n). Since
the sequences (un, vn) take on their values in a ﬁnite set, after extracting
subsequences, if necessary, we may, and do, assume that they are constant,
say equal to (u, v) and (u′, v′), respectively. By letting n tend to inﬁnity in
the equality
χρn(u, v) = χρn(u′, v′),
we obtain (u, v) = (u′, v′), a contradiction.
For the surjectivity of χρ, the reasoning is similar. If, for a sequence ρn that
tends to inﬁnity, there exists an ℓn that is not in the image of χρn, then we
ﬁrst show that lim ℓn = (u, v) for some pair (u, v) ∈MΓ ′(x, y) × MΓ ′′(y, z)
(y ∈Crit AHb, µ(y) = µ(x)). Next we apply a uniqueness property of the
gluing (like that of Section 9.6) to show that ℓn ∈Im χn for large n and
arrive at a contradiction.
Here are the details of this proof.

Proposition 11.5.6.
(1) Let (sn) be a sequence of real numbers. Then the sequence ℓn(s + sn, t)
admits a convergent subsequence for the C∞

(2) Let ℓbe the limit of such a subsequence. There exists a constant s⋆such

the pairs (Ha, Ja), (H′, J′), (Hb, Jb), (H′′, J′′), (Hc, Jc).
Proof of Proposition 11.5.6. Let us ﬁrst prove point (2), admitting point (1).
The map (s, t) 7→ℓn(s+sn, t) is a solution of the Floer equation corresponding
to the pair
(Hn, Jn) = (Hρn(s + sn, t, p), Jρn(s + sn, p)).
Recall that, by deﬁnition, Hn and Jn are given by
Hn(s, t, p) =
(

if s ≤−sn

if s ≥−sn

(

if s ≤−sn
J′′(s −ρn + sn, p)
if s ≥−sn.
Let us study the diﬀerent possible limits of (Hn, Jn) (for the C∞
loc topology)
in terms of the behavior of the sequence (sn).
In the special case where sn = −ρn, it is obvious that (Hn, Jn) tends to
(H′, J′). Likewise, when sn = ρn, (Hn, Jn) tends to (H′′, J′′). The limit ℓwill
therefore be a solution of the Floer equation corresponding to Γ ′ = (H′, J′)
in the ﬁrst case and to Γ ′′ = (H′′, J′′) in the second case.
Since the statement of (2) allows us to add a convergent sequence to (sn),
we only need to study the case where the sequences (sn ± ρn) are divergent:
• If lim(sn + ρn) = −∞, that is, if sn ≪−ρn, then we easily obtain
lim
n→+∞(Hn, Jn) = (Ha, Ja).
• If limn→+∞(sn + ρn) = +∞and limn→+∞(sn −ρn) = −∞, in other
words, if −ρn ≪sn ≪ρn, then by analyzing separately the cases where

460
11 Floer Homology: Invariance
(sn) tends to −∞, is convergent or tends to +∞, we ﬁnd that
lim
n→+∞(Hn, Jn) = (Hb, Jb).
• If lim(sn −ρn) = +∞, that is, if sn ≫ρn, then we have
lim
n→+∞(Hn, Jn) = (Hc, Jc).
We have thus proved point (2); let us now move on to point (1). We ﬁrst copy
the proof of Proposition 6.6.2 (a bound on the gradient in the proof of the
compactness theorem) to prove that there exists a constant A > 0 such that
∀ρ, ∀ℓ∈MΓρ(x, z), ∀(s, t) ∈R × S1,

(here ∥·∥denotes an arbitrary norm on TW—they are all equivalent be-

If such a constant did not exist, then as in the proof of Proposition 6.6.2, we
would obtain a J-holomorphic curve v, which would lead to a contradiction
as in Lemma 6.6.4. The structure J in question is the limit (for the C∞
loc
topology) of a sequence Jρn. It is therefore either equal to Jρ⋆for a ρ⋆> 0
(and in this case depends on s), or equal to Jb. The inequality above is
therefore satisﬁed by ℓn for every n and also by the elements of the sequence
(ℓn(s + sn, t)). The latter form an equicontinuous family, which allows us to
apply the Arzel`a–Ascoli theorem and to ﬁnd a subsequence that converges
to a limit ℓfor the C0
loc topology.
Point (2) then shows that there exists a constant s⋆∈R such that ℓ(s+s⋆)
is a solution, in the weak sense, of one of the Floer equations stated in the
proposition. Elliptic regularity (Lemma 12.1.1) then implies that ℓis of class
C∞and that the convergence of ℓn(s + sn, t) to ℓindeed holds in the C∞
loc
sense.

Remark 11.5.7. Note that we cannot exclude solutions of the Floer equation
that are constant with respect to s, because we cannot exclude the case where

equal (without the homotopy connecting the pairs necessarily being trivial).
We therefore need to consider the case where x, y and z are not all distinct.

uniformly for s ∈[−A, A].

B(z, δ), which allows us to deﬁne Yn using Lemma 9.6.11, as before.
This concludes the proof when u and v are not constant in s. If one of the
two solutions (or both) is (are) constant, then the proof is simpler. Suppose,
for example, that v is constant in s (in particular, we then have y = z). The
proof of Theorem 9.1.7, which constructs the broken limit trajectory of ℓn,
then implies that ℓn(s) ∈B(z, δ) for every s > s⋆−ρn (for some constant s⋆).
The pre-gluing wρn(s) obviously has the same property since for s ≥−ρn+A,
u(s + ρn) ∈B(y, δ) = B(z, δ)
and
v(s −ρn) = y = z ∈B(z, δ).
It will therefore suﬃce to deﬁne Yn for the intervals described in (1) and (2),
which can be done in an analogous manner.

11.5 Conclusion of the Proof of the Invariance
467
The proof of Proposition 11.5.11 is exactly the same as that of Proposi-
tion 11.2.4 and its analogue Proposition 9.6.4 (in Subsection 9.6.c). The fact
that

ρTW)
follows from (the analogue of) Lemma 9.6.13 (proved in the same manner).
To show that lim ∥Yn∥W 1,p = 0, we make an estimate, as in the proof
of Proposition 11.2.4, using (the analogue of) Lemma 13.7.1. Note that
Lemma 9.6.14 is useful here (because µ(x) = µ(y) = µ(z), hence Wn = 0); it
makes it easier to obtain the estimate of the W 1,p norm of Yn.
⊓⊔
End of the Proof of Proposition 11.5.9. We want to show that for n suﬃ-
ciently large,
ℓn = χρn(u, v),
in other words,
expwρn Yn = expwρn γρn,
that is, Yn = γρn. We know that
Fρn(Yn) = Fρn(γρn) = 0
and that Lρn = (dFρn)0 is bijective, as we saw in Section 11.5.a. But we can-
not apply the local inversion theorem directly because the size of the neigh-
borhood on which Fρn is bijective could decrease when n increases and Yn is
not necessarily contained in this neighborhood. We use the uniqueness lemma
9.6.16 that states that the solution γρn of Fρn(Y ) = 0 obtained through the
Newton–Picard method is the only one in the ball B(0, ε0), where the con-
stant ε0 does not depend on n.
Consequently, since the W 1,p norm of Yn tends to 0, we have Yn = γρn,
concluding the proof of Proposition 11.5.9.
⊓⊔
We therefore obtain the surjectivity of χρ for ρ suﬃciently large, which we
needed to conclude the proof of Proposition 11.5.3.
⊓⊔
This trivially implies Proposition 11.5.2: Lemma 11.5.1 asserts that Γρ is
regular for arbitrarily large values of ρ and Proposition 11.5.3 implies that,
from a certain value of ρ,
ΦΓρ = ΦΓ ′ ◦ΦΓ ′′
if the left-hand side is deﬁned.

PDF PAGE 478
468
11 Floer Homology: Invariance
Finally, Proposition 11.5.2 obviously implies Proposition 11.2.9, which was
the object of this section. Propositions 11.2.8 and 11.2.9 imply the invariance
of the Floer homology with respect to the choice of a regular pair (H, J).
11.6 Conclusion
We have achieved the aims of the plan announced in Section 6.2 (p. 160) and
proved the Arnold conjecture.
We must point out that Floer homology and its diﬀerent relatives have
many other applications to symplectic geometry and contact geometry than
this conjecture. In fact, the second part of this book would be the starting
point for readers interested in modern aspects of symplectic topology. Here
are some directions and references for those who would like to go further.
First of all, there is the study of the Lagrangian submanifolds of symplectic
manifolds,3 already undertaken by Floer in [29]; see also the works of Oh [59,
60, 61], Seidel and Fukaya [34, 69, 70], and, closer to us, those of Damian [22]
and Gadbled [36].
Next, there is the generalization of our construction in the case of symplec-
tic manifolds with contact-type boundaries. In this case, the Floer homology
is no longer isomorphic to the Morse homology as it was here, but takes into
account the closed characteristics of the boundary. This leads to the symplec-
tic homology developed by Viterbo [77] (see also the article by Oancea [58],
and that by Cieliebak and Frauenfelder [20]).
Another branch goes toward the study of contact manifolds. A variant of
the Floer construction for the symplectizations of contact manifolds gives a
“contact homology” generated by the periodic trajectories of the Reeb ﬁelds
of the manifold. This theory is due to Eliashberg, Givental and Hofer [27].
See also the works of Bourgeois [15].
But this could ﬁll another book.
3 We would like to update this list of references, adding especially the recent books and
papers of some of the leading experts in the ﬁeld [35, 71, 33, 10, 11], as well as the new
results obtained by one of the authors [23, 24].

PDF PAGE 479
Chapter 12
The Elliptic Regularity of the Floer
Operator
12.1 Elliptic Regularity: Why and How?
We have invoked several times, under the name “elliptic regularity”, the
regularity of the solutions of the Floer equation
∂u
∂s + J(u)∂u
∂t + grad Ht(u) = 0.
The ﬁrst time, in Chapter 6, we needed it to show the compactness of the
space M of ﬁnite energy solutions (more precisely, Proposition 6.5.3). The
statement we used is the following.
Lemma 12.1.1 (Elliptic Regularity).
Let U be an open subset of C.
Every sequence (un) of solutions of class C∞of the Floer equation
∂u
∂s + J(u)∂u
∂t + grad Ht(u) = 0
deﬁned on U and satisfying
sup
n∈N
∥grad un∥L∞(U) ≤M
(for some real M) has a subsequence that converges uniformly on every com-
pact subset of U, as do all its derivatives, to a limit that is therefore of
class C∞.
Next, in the proof of the transversality theorem 8.1.2 on p. 265, we also
called on elliptic regularity to justify the fact that the solutions of the Floer
equation in the Sobolev-type space P1,p(x, y) are of class C∞and that the
topology induced by that of P1,p on this space of solutions is the same as that
of M, that is, the C∞
loc topology (P1,p(x, y) is deﬁned in Deﬁnition 8.2.2).
469

PDF PAGE 480
470
12 Elliptic Regularity
After that, the elliptic regularity argument appeared in similar situations:
in Chapter 9, to prove that the P1,p solutions produced by gluing are
“true” solutions and converge to a broken trajectory in the right topology
(Lemma 9.4.17), and then in Chapter 11, where compactness, transversality
and gluing results are proved in a more general setting, where the almost
complex structure J and the Hamiltonian H depend on s and sometimes on
another parameter λ.
We therefore see that elliptic regularity plays a role in all steps of the
construction of the Floer homology. This is why we will now give rigorous
statements and detailed proofs of these properties. We have mainly used [51].

12.1.b (Nonlinear) Elliptic Regularity Results
“Nonlinear” elliptic regularity properties are included in the following state-
ment.
Proposition 12.1.4. For p > 2, a solution u ∈W 1,p
loc (R × S1; W) of the
Floer equation is of class C∞. On the space of solutions W 1,p
loc (R × S1; W),
the W 1,p
loc and C∞
loc topologies coincide.
The notation W 1,p
loc (R×S1; W) is new in this book; we will clarify its mean-
ing later. Also note that, contrary to the earlier statements, nonlinear elliptic
regularity needs the assumption that p > 2. Here are some explanations.
Why Assume That p > 2?
In all regularity statements, the aim is to prove that a weak solution (so-
lution in the sense of distributions) is a true solution of class C∞. In our
case, by assumption, the solution is in the Sobolev space W 1,p(R × S1). As
we explained in Section 8.2, assuming that p > 2 guarantees, via Rellich’s
theorem (Theorem C.4.6), that this solution is continuous. At the same time,
this allows us to work with local coordinates.
The regularity of a solution u : R × S1 →W can be read in charts of W.
But other properties that are involved here, for example Lp integrability (even
on compact sets), are not invariant under transition maps. On the other hand,
if u is in W 1,p(R × S1; R2n) and if ϕ : R2n →R2n is continuous, then for
p > 2, ϕ ◦u ∈W 1,p
loc (R × S1; R2n). Indeed, ϕ ◦u is in Lp
loc(R × S1; R2n) by
the continuity of u, and
∂(ϕ ◦u)
∂s
= (dϕ)u
∂u
∂s

∈Lp
loc(R × S1; R2n)
(and likewise for the derivative with respect to t) because it is a product
of a continuous function and an Lp
loc function. Consequently, we can deﬁne
the space W 1,p(R × S1; W) as the subspace of continuous functions that, in
coordinates, are represented by W 1,p(R × S1; R2n) functions. An equivalent
alternative is to embed W in Rm and to consider the W 1,p
loc (R × S1; Rm)

PDF PAGE 483
12.1 Elliptic Regularity: Why and How?
473
functions that take on their values in W (the deﬁnition will not depend on
the chosen embedding).
This is not the only reason to assume that p > 2. The main ingredient
of the proof of all these regularity statements is an “elliptic bootstrapping”
argument that asserts that if a solution is in W k,p
loc (R×S1; R2n), then it is also
in W k+1,p
loc
(R×S1; R2n). The regularity of the solution is then a consequence
of Theorem C.4.9. We applied this argument in the proof of linear elliptic
regularity (Theorem 12.1.3). In the nonlinear case, the condition p > 2 turns
out to be crucial. See the proof of Theorem 12.1.5 in Section 12.4.c.
The proof of Lemma 12.1.1 relies on Proposition 12.1.4, but it is not an
immediate consequence of it. This is because applying the Arzel`a–Ascoli the-

that this limit is in W 1,p

latter will be proved in Section 12.4.
Proposition 12.1.4 gives the regularity argument needed in the proof of

tained in W 1,p

13.4 Proof of the Two Lemmas: the First One
Let us ﬁnally prove the two lemmas.
Proof of Lemma 9.4.8. To apply the formula for Fρ established in Sec-
tion 13.3, we need a result that asserts that the property announced in
Lemma 9.4.8 is stable under the composition of operators. This is taken care
of by the following lemma, where ϕ and ψ denote maps between Banach
spaces that are not necessarily linear but are diﬀerentiable (we will often
denote this type of object by the word “operator”).
Lemma 13.4.1. Let G, H and K be Banach spaces and let ϕ : G →H and
ψ : H →K be diﬀerentiable maps. We suppose there that exist an r0 > 0
and ki > 0 (for i = 1, . . . , 4) such that:
(1) For every Z ∈B(0, r0) ⊂G, ∥(dϕ)Z −(dϕ)0∥op ≤k1 ∥Z∥.
(2) ∥(dϕ)0∥op ≤k2.
(3) For every Z ∈B(0, r0) ⊂G,
(dψ)ϕ(Z) −(dψ)ϕ(0)
op ≤k3 ∥ϕ(Z) −ϕ(0)∥.
(4)
(dψ)ϕ(0)
op ≤k4.
Then there exists a k > 0 that depends only on the ki and on r0, such that
for every Z ∈B(0, r0), we have
∥(dψ)Z ◦ϕ −(dψ)0 ◦ϕ∥op ≤k ∥Z∥.
We will apply this lemma to operators ϕ and ψ that depend on the param-
eter ρ. This is why we included the conditions on the norms of the diﬀerentials
of these operators in the assumptions: we would like to obtain a constant k
that does not depend on ρ.

The second lemma (used in the proof of the uniqueness of the gluing) that
we want to prove here is the following.
Lemma (Lemma 9.6.15). There exist positive constants K1 and K2 such
that for every n ∈N,
∥Yn∥W 1,p ≤K1
∥χn∥W 1,p + ∥Yn∥L∞+ ∥eF(0)∥Lp
1 −K2 ∥Yn∥L∞
.
We of course use the notation deﬁned near Lemma 9.6.15. Recall that
ζn ∈W 1,p(R × S1; R2n) can be decomposed into χn + ωn, where χn is of the
form α#wnβ (α ∈Ker Lu, β ∈Ker Lv) and ωn is orthogonal to the vectors
of this form (see p. 366).

Proof. The constants Ci below are independent of n. We have
∥Yn∥W 1,p = ∥i(ζn)∥W 1,p ≤C1 ∥ζn∥W 1,p = C1 ∥χn + ωn∥W 1,p
≤C1 (∥χn∥W 1,p + ∥ωn∥W 1,p) ,
where we have used the fact that ∥i∥is bounded (independently of n), which
we proved on p. 496. We apply Proposition 9.4.7 to ωn ∈W ⊥
n and ﬁnd
∥Yn∥W 1,p ≤C1 (∥χn∥W 1,p + ∥ωn∥W 1,p)
≤C1 ∥χn∥W 1,p + C2 ∥L(ωn)∥Lp
= C1 ∥χn∥W 1,p + C2 ∥L(ζn −χn)∥Lp
≤C1 ∥χn∥W 1,p + C2 ∥L(ζn)∥Lp + C2 ∥L(χn)∥Lp .
The norm of the linear operator L is bounded from above by a constant
that does not depend on n. Indeed, L = (dF)0 for F = p ◦(Id ×eF) ◦i. For
the operators that occur in this composition, we have veriﬁed conditions (2)
and (4) of Lemma 13.5.1, exactly what we need to conclude that the norm
∥(dF)0∥of the diﬀerential of the composition is uniformly bounded. We must
however stress that these results were obtained for the pre-gluing wρ and not
for a pre-gluing of the form wν,ρ. The readers can verify that in the more
general case, the arguments are similar in all details.
Let us return to the estimate we just obtained for the norm of Yn. From
the above, we deduce that
∥Yn∥W 1,p ≤C1 ∥χn∥W 1,p + C2 ∥L(ζn)∥Lp + C3 ∥χn∥W 1,p
= (C1 + C3) ∥χn∥W 1,p + C2 ∥L(ζn)∥Lp .
By Lemma 9.6.14, we know that the ﬁrst term tends to 0. We still need to
bound the Lp norm of L(ζn). We have
L(ζn) = (dF)0(ζn) = (d(p ◦(Id ×eF) ◦i))0(ζn)
= (d(p ◦(Id ×eF)))0i(ζn)
= (d(p ◦(Id ×eF)))0(Yn)
= (D1p)(0,eF(0))(Yn) + (D2p)(0,eF(0))(deF)0(Yn).
The map p = pρ (which now depends on n),
