Chapter 14
Exercises for the Second Part
As its title may suggest, this chapter contains exercises on the second part
(of this book).
14.1 Exercises on Chapter 5
Exercise 1. Let E be a symplectic vector space of dimension 2n. If F is a
subset of E, then we let F ◦denote the orthogonal vector subspace for the
symplectic form, which consists of all vectors x such that ω(x, y) = 0 for
every y ∈F.
For a subspace F of E, show that
dim F + dim F ◦= dim E.
We call F isotropic if the form ω is zero on F, that is, if F ⊂F ◦. What can
we say about the dimension of F? We call F Lagrangian if F is isotropic and
dim F = n.
Let F be a “co-isotropic” subspace (that is, one whose orthogonal is
isotropic). Show that ω induces a symplectic form on the quotient vector
space F/F ◦. Let L be a Lagrangian subspace of E such that L + F = E.
Show that the composition
L ∩F ⊂F −→F/F ◦
is the injection of a Lagrangian subspace.
Exercise 2. Let V be a manifold and let η be a 1-form on V . We can view η
as a section
η : V −→T ⋆V
535

PDF PAGE 546
536
14 Exercises for the Second Part
of the cotangent bundle. If λ denotes the Liouville form on T ⋆V , then what
is the form η⋆λ on V ?
Exercise 3 (Lagrangian submanifolds). If (W, ω) is a symplectic mani-
fold of dimension 2n, then we call a submanifold j : L ⊂W Lagrangian if
dim L = n and j⋆ω = 0.
(1) Prove that in a symplectic surface, all curves are Lagrangian.
(2) Prove that if L1 is Lagrangian in W1 and L2 is Lagrangian in W2, then
L1 × L2 is Lagrangian in W1 × W2. Construct a Lagrangian torus T n in
R2n. Prove that every symplectic manifold contains Lagrangian tori.
(3) Let η be a 1-form on a manifold V . We view η as a section of T ⋆V (as in
Exercise 2 on p. 535). Prove that the image of η is a Lagrangian subman-
ifold if and only if the form η is closed.
(4) Let f be a C∞function on a compact manifold V . By the above, the 1-
form df deﬁnes a Lagrangian submanifold of T ⋆V . Prove that it meets the
zero section.
(5) Consider the product manifold W × W endowed with the symplectic form
ω ⊕(−ω). Prove that the diagonal is a Lagrangian submanifold. Let ϕ be
a diﬀeomorphism from W into itself. Under what condition on ϕ is the
graph of ϕ a Lagrangian submanifold of W × W?
Exercise 4. Let w be a C∞map from the sphere S2 to the cotangent space
T ⋆V of a manifold V . Show that
Z
S2 w⋆ω = 0.
Exercise 5. Let ω be a symplectic form on a compact surface Σ. Prove
that Σ is oriented, and then that
Z
Σ
ω ̸= 0.
Consider the inclusion P1(C) ⊂Pn(C) induced by the inclusion C2 ⊂Cn+1.
Prove that it is a symplectic submanifold. Deduce that there exists a C∞map
w : S2 −→Pn(C)
such that if ω now denotes the symplectic form of Pn(C), then
Z
S2 w⋆ω ̸= 0.

Exercise 6. Let ϕ1 be a Hamiltonian diﬀeomorphism of W. Show that the
graph of ϕ1 is transversal to the diagonal of W × W at (x, x) if and only if
the trajectory of x is nondegenerate.
Exercise 7 (The Group of Hamiltonian Diﬀeomorphisms). In this ex-
ercise we consider a compact manifold W endowed with a symplectic form ω.
Let Ham(W) denote the set of diﬀeomorphisms of W that are Hamiltonian
ﬂows at time 1.

Gt = Ht + Kt ◦ϕ−1

Ham(W) is a normal subgroup of the group of all symplectic diﬀeomor-
phisms of W.

Exercise 9 (Harmonic Oscillator). On the symplectic manifold R2n, we

Exercise 10. Let E be a vector space endowed with a symplectic form ω and

only if L⊥= JL.
Exercise 11. Let (W, ω) be a symplectic manifold and let J be an almost
complex structure calibrated by ω. Let V ⊂W be a complex submanifold,
that is, one that is stable under J:
∀x ∈V,
Jx(TxV ) ⊂TxV.
Verify that ω deﬁnes a nondegenerate form on V .
Exercise 12 (Complex, but not Symplectic). Consider the map
f : C2 −→C2
(z1, z2) 7−→(2z1, 2z2)

n · (z1, z2) = f n(z1, z2).
Show that the quotient is a complex manifold (that is, with complex analytic
transition maps) that is diﬀeomorphic to S3×S1. Consequently, its second de
Rham cohomology group is zero. Deduce that H does not have any symplectic
structure.2

Exercise 13 (Cayley Numbers and Almost Complex Structures).
Recall that the algebra of Cayley numbers or octaves is a real vector space O
of dimension 8, endowed with a basis that we denote by
(1, i, j, k, ℓ, ℓi, ℓj, ℓk)
and with multiplication (that is right and left distributive over addition)
deﬁned by
i2 = j2 = k2 = ℓ2 = ijk = jki = kij = −1,
iℓ= −ℓi, etc.
Recall that this multiplication is neither commutative nor associative (be-
cause (ij)ℓ= −i(jℓ)), but that it does satisfy (ab)b = a(bb) for all a and b.
Consider the Euclidean space R7 of imaginary octaves, that is, the sub-
space generated by (i, j, k, ℓi, ℓj, ℓk). Let V ⊂R7 be an oriented submanifold
of dimension 6. For any point x of V , we let n(x) denote the unitary normal
vector (deﬁned by the orientations of V and R7) and we deﬁne
Jx : TxV −→R7
by setting Jx(u) = n(x) · u (multiplication in the sense of octaves). Show
that Jx has values in TxV and that the endomorphisms Jx deﬁne an almost
complex structure J on V . In this way, all hypersurfaces of R7 are almost
complex.3

∀t,

Exercise 17 (Symplectization of a Contact Manifold). Let V be a
manifold endowed with a 1-form α that is nonsingular (that is, such that

We call α a contact form on V . The dimension of V must be odd; we write
it as 2n + 1.
(1) Show that α ∧(dα)∧n is a volume form on V .
(2) Show that there exists a unique vector ﬁeld X on V such that
iXα ≡1
and
iX(dα) = 0
(we call X the Reeb vector ﬁeld of α).
(3) Consider W = V × R endowed with the 2-form ω deﬁned by
ω(x,σ) = d(eσα).
Show that (W, ω) is a symplectic manifold (called the symplectization of
the contact manifold (V, α)).
(4) Determine the Hamiltonian vector ﬁeld of the function H(x, σ) = σ.
Example.
Let V = S2n+1 ⊂Cn+1 be the unit sphere (Cn+1 is endowed with coordinates
(q1 + ip1, . . . , qn+1 + ipn+1)) and consider the 1-form
α = 1
2
X
(pidqi −qidpi).

of α. Prove that the symplectization of (S2n+1, α) is symplectomorphic to
Cn+1 −{0} endowed with its standard symplectic form.

Appendix A
A Bit of Diﬀerential Geometry
In this chapter, we list, more or less succinctly depending on the case, the
diﬀerent objects and tools from diﬀerential geometry used in this book.
A.1 Manifolds and Submanifolds
See [45], [48], [47], [74] and [63] for the basic notions set out in this section.
A.1.a Manifolds
A manifold is a space that, locally, greatly resembles Rn. Let us be more
precise. A topological manifold of dimension n is a separated topological
space such that every point is contained in an open subset homeomorphic to
an open subset of Rn. A pair (U, ϕ), where U is such an open space and ϕ is
a homeomorphism, is a local coordinate system or chart. The manifolds we
consider will be at least paracompact, and in particular separable.
We also require that V be a countable union of compact sets and a count-
able union of open sets of charts. In this book, we will mostly use compact
manifolds, for which these properties automatically hold.
The set of charts of a manifold is called an atlas. Two charts (U, ϕ) and
(U ′, ϕ′) are called compatible if the composition
ϕ(U ∩U ′)
ϕ−1
−−−−→U ∩U ′
ϕ′
−−−→ϕ′(U ∩U ′),
which maps one open subset of Rn to another, is a diﬀeomorphism (of
class C∞). If the manifold V has an atlas whose charts are mutually compat-
ible, then we call V a diﬀerential manifold (or a smooth or C∞manifold).
555

PDF PAGE 566
556
A A Bit of Diﬀerential Geometry
Examples. The sphere Sn, the projective spaces Pn(R) and Pn(C) and the
torus Rn/Zn are (smooth) manifolds.
Using this deﬁnition, a zero-dimensional manifold is a discrete countable
set.
Using charts and the ad hoc notion on the open subsets of Rn, we deﬁne the
notion of C∞map from one manifold to another: if U and V are contained in
open subsets of charts on manifolds M and N, respectively, then the function
f : U →V is of class Ck (for 0 ≤k ≤∞) if the composition
ϕ(U)
ϕ−1
−−−−→U
f
−−→V
ψ
−−→ψ(V )
is of class Ck. The class Ck property is local and is well deﬁned by this
condition.
A.1.b Characterization of Submanifolds
Among the manifolds, there are submanifolds of Rn. A submanifold of Rn is a
subspace V that, locally, greatly resembles a linear subspace in the sense that
each of its points has an open neighborhood U in Rn that is diﬀeomorphic
to a neighborhood of 0 in Rn by a diﬀeomorphism ϕ such that ϕ(U ∩V ) =
ϕ(U) ∩Rd. The integer d is the dimension of the submanifold V .
Theorem A.1.1. Let V ⊂Rn. The following properties are equivalent:
(1) V is a submanifold of dimension d of Rn.
(2) Every point x of V has an open neighborhood U in Rn such that there
exists a submersion g : U →Rn−d with U ∩V = g−1(0).
(3) Every point x of V has an open neighborhood U in Rn such that there
exist a neighborhood Ωof 0 in Rd and an immersion h : Ω→Rn that is
a homeomorphism from Ωonto U ∩V .
In other words, a submanifold can be described locally by equations (where
the number of equations is the codimension) or by parametrization (where the
number of parameters is the dimension). All these properties are consequences
of the implicit function theorem and/or of the local inversion theorem.
An excellent and indispensable exercise is to decide, given in Rn or in
another manifold a subset suspected of being a submanifold, which of these
criteria is best sequenced for proving its guilt.
The deﬁnition of a submanifold of a manifold is analogous.
Of course, submanifolds are manifolds (this is included in the deﬁnition).
For example, a “level set” of a function with real values, that is, the inverse
image of a real number α, falls under property (2). It is a submanifold when f
is a submersion along f −1(α).

PDF PAGE 567
A.1 Manifolds and Submanifolds
557
The converse also holds: manifolds can be considered as submanifolds, as
we will now recall.
A.1.c Partitions of Unity, Embedding Theorems
Partitions of Unity.
This is an essential tool, which allows us to prove many results by concen-
trating on their local aspects. Let V be a compact manifold1 of dimension n
and let (Ui, ϕi)1≤i≤N be a ﬁnite atlas of V . We can show that there exist a
cover of V by open sets Ωi such that Ωi ⊂Ui and functions hi with support
in Ui and value 1 on Ωi.
Setting
pi =
hi
P hi
,
we deduce that for every cover of V by ﬁnitely many open sets Ω1, . . . , ΩN,
there exists a family (pi)1≤i≤N of functions
pi : V −→[0, +∞[
such that
Supp(pi) ⊂Ωi
and
N
X
i=1
pi ≡1.
Embedding of a compact manifold in a Euclidean space.
Let V be a compact manifold of dimension n and let (Ui, ϕi)1≤i≤N be a ﬁnite
atlas of V . We use the functions hi introduced above to construct a map F
deﬁned by
F = (h1ϕ1, . . . , hNϕN, h1, . . . , hN) : V −→(Rn)N × RN = R(n+1)N.
We can verify that F is an injective immersion, and therefore an embedding;
see the following theorem.
Theorem A.1.2 (Whitney embedding theorem).
Let V be a compact
manifold; then there exists an embedding of V as a submanifold in a Euclidean
space RN.

Using a general position argument similar to the one we will recall later,
we can prove that the dimension of the Euclidean space can be reduced to

A A Bit of Diﬀerential Geometry
Remark. This result belongs to real diﬀerential geometry; it does not have
an analogue in complex analytic geometry: by Liouville’s theorem, no com-
pact analytical manifold is an analytic submanifold of CN.
A.1.d Boundaries
A manifold with boundary is a topological space V such that every point has
a neighborhood homeomorphic to an open subset of the half-space (xn ≤0)
in Rn. The points that do not have a neighborhood homeomorphic to an
open subset of Rn are the boundary points. The boundary, denoted by ∂V ,
is a submanifold of dimension n −1. We can prove (see Section A.4) that the
boundary has a neighborhood of the form ∂V × [0, ε[.
A.1.e Tangent Vectors, Tangent Maps
Let us recall that we can deﬁne a vector tangent to the manifold V at x as
an equivalence class of curves on V passing through x, namely
c : ] −ε, ε[ −→V
such that c(0) = x,
for the equivalence relation that identiﬁes c1 and c2 if for a chart ϕ centered
at x, we have
(ϕ ◦c1)′(0) = (ϕ ◦c2)′(0).
The set of vectors tangent at x is a vector space that we denote by TxV .
This allows us to deﬁne the tangent map
Txf : TxV −→Tf(x)W
associated with a map f : V →W, simply by sending the class of the curve c
to that of the curve f ◦c.
It also allows us to consider a tangent vector as a derivation on the func-
tions, that is, as a linear map X deﬁned on the set of C∞functions in the
neighborhood of x and satisfying
X(fg) = f(x)X(g) + g(x)X(f).
For every x, given a vector X ∈TxV and a function f : V →R, we let
X(f)(x) = Txf(X) ∈R; this deﬁnes the function X(f).
Remark A.1.3. This function is often denoted by (df)x instead of Txf, in
particular when f is a function with real values. We will do so here.

PDF PAGE 569
A.1 Manifolds and Submanifolds
559
Describing the tangent space of a submanifold using each of the character-
izations is quite instructive (in particular, we ﬁnd that Tx(f −1(0)) = Ker Txf
when f is submersive).
A.1.f Vector Fields
We also deﬁne the tangent bundle2 by putting the topology and ad hoc struc-
ture of manifold on the disjoint union of the tangent spaces at the points of
the manifold.
The vector ﬁelds are C∞sections3 of the tangent bundle. In other words, a
vector ﬁeld consists of, for every point, a tangent vector that depends in a C∞
manner on the point in question. In general, we will use Roman capitals such
as X to denote vector ﬁelds, while Xx will denote the value of the ﬁeld X at
the point x.
If X is a vector ﬁeld and f is a function, then we denote by X · f the
derivative of f along X deﬁned by
(X · f)(x) = Xx · f = Txf(Xx).
All derivations arise from vector ﬁelds.
Lie Bracket.
The composition of two derivations is not a derivation; however,
f 7−→X · (Y · f) −Y · (X · f)
is one. We denote the corresponding vector ﬁeld by [X, Y ].
A vector ﬁeld can also be considered as a diﬀerential equation on the
manifold. See Section A.4.
A.1.g Diﬀerential Forms
A diﬀerential form of degree p on a manifold V (also called a p-form) is a
section of the bundle ΛpT ⋆V . In less pretentious terms, it consists of, for
every x ∈V , an exterior derivative of degree p on the tangent space TxV
that depends in a C∞manner on x. In local coordinates (x1, . . . , xn) on V ,
this can be written as
α =
X
αi1,...,ipdxi1 ∧· · · ∧dxip,
2 And, likewise, the cotangent bundle, by using the duals of the tangent spaces.
3 A section of a vector bundle π : E →V is a map s : V →E such that π ◦s = IdV .

PDF PAGE 570
560
A A Bit of Diﬀerential Geometry
where the αi1,...,ip are functions and the dx1, . . . , dxn denote the canonical
basis of (Rn)⋆.
A map f : V →W sends diﬀerential forms on W to diﬀerential forms
on V through
(f ⋆α)x(X1, . . . , Xp) = αf(x)(Txf(X1), . . . , Txf(Xp)).
The most important operation on diﬀerential forms is exterior diﬀerentia-
tion. It transforms a p-form into a (p + 1)-form; in coordinates, we have
dα =
X
dαi1,...,ip ∧dxi1 ∧· · · ∧dxip
(where dαi1,...,ip is the diﬀerential of the function αi1,...,ip).
It satisﬁes d ◦d = 0. There are closed forms (dα = 0) and exact forms
(α = dβ). The exact forms are closed, but the converse does not hold in
general, giving rise to de Rham cohomology.
We will in particular need 1-forms and 2-forms, the exterior derivative,
closed forms, exact forms, the notion of volume form and Stokes’s theorem.
See [45, Chapter V] and [74, Chapter VII].
A.1.h Matters of Orientation
We call a manifold orientable if it has an oriented atlas (such that all of its
transition maps preserve the orientation). A manifold with a volume form
is orientable (the proof is immediate). The converse is also true, we can
construct a volume form using a partition of unity (see [45, Chapter V] if the
manifold is compact and [37, p. 46] in the general case).
The boundary of an oriented manifold has a natural orientation.
Example. If f : Rn →R is a submersion, then the submanifold f −1(0) is
orientable. To show this, we can use the gradient of the function, which is a
vector ﬁeld that is normal to the submanifold.
A.2 Critical Points, Critical Values and Sard’s Theorem
See for example [39] for this section.
A.2.a Critical Points
A point x of the manifold M is called critical for the map
f : M −→N

PDF PAGE 571
A.2 Critical Points, Critical Values and Sard’s Theorem
561
with values in the manifold N if the linear map Txf does not have maximal
rank:
• Either dim M ≤dim N and f is not an immersion at x
• Or dim M ≥dim N and f is not a submersion at x.
Let m and n be the dimensions of M and N, respectively and alphabetically.
When m ≤n, the condition is dim Ker Txf > 0; when m ≥n, it is rk Txf < n.
We can summarize this by saying that x is critical if the corank of f at x is
positive, using the ad hoc deﬁnition of corank, that is, inf(m, n) −rk Txf.
Example (basic example). When N = R, we have n = 1 and a point x
is critical if and only if Txf ≡0.
A.2.b Sard’s Theorem
A critical value of the map f is a point of the target manifold N that is
the image of a critical point. The other points of N are regular values. We
should note, in particular, that a regular value is not necessarily a value, that
is, is not necessarily contained in the image of f. Moreover, there can be
noncritical points whose images are critical values.
Sard’s theorem states that under certain conditions, almost all values of
a diﬀerentiable map are regular (note that the notion of negligible set and
therefore that of “almost everywhere” are well deﬁned on a manifold). More
precisely, we have the following result.
Theorem A.2.1 (Sard’s theorem).
Let f : M →N be a C∞map; then
the set of critical values of f has measure zero in N.
Remark A.2.2. When the dimension m of M is less than n, the dimension
of N, then f(M) itself has measure zero in N. This is a not entirely trivial
consequence of the fact that the subspace Rm has measure zero in Rn. For
example, in this case, a diﬀerentiable map from M to N is never surjective.
The theorem is therefore above all interesting (and more diﬃcult to prove)
when m ≥n.
See for example [39, p. 34ﬀ.] for a proof.
A.2.c Regular Level Sets of a Function, Sublevel Sets
If f :V →R is a function and if α is a regular value of f, then by the reg-
ular value theorem (that is, characterization (2) of Theorem A.1.1), the
level set f −1(α) is a submanifold of codimension 1 of V . The sublevel set
V α = f −1(] −∞, α]) is, in turn, a manifold with boundary f −1(α).

PDF PAGE 572
562
A A Bit of Diﬀerential Geometry
Let f : (V, ∂V ) →W be a C∞map from a manifold with boundary to a
manifold W. We suppose that the point w of W is a regular value for both f
and f|∂V ; then f −1(w) is a submanifold of V , with boundary
∂f −1(w) = (f|∂V )−1 (w).
A.3 Transversality
This notion, due to Thom at the beginning of the 1950s, is essential in ge-
ometry. It concerns the relative position of two submanifolds.
A.3.a First notion of transversality
The story begins with (yet) another application of the implicit function the-
orem.
Theorem A.3.1. Let M and N be two submanifolds of a manifold P. Let u
be a point of M ∩N such that
TuM + TuN = TuP.
Then in the neighborhood of u, the intersection M ∩N is a submanifold
of P. Its codimension is the sum of the codimensions of M and of N, and its
tangent space at u is
Tu(M ∩N) = TuM ∩TuN.
We have assumed that everything is of class C∞, but class C1 suﬃces.
Proof. The statement is local in the neighborhood of u. Let us therefore
suppose that P = Rp. Let m be the codimension of M and let n be that
of N, so that, always in the neighborhood of u, we may also assume that
M = f −1(0) and N = g−1(0) for submersions f and g. The condition of
general position on the tangent spaces can then be written as
dim(Ker Tuf + Ker Tug) = p.
Let us therefore consider the map F = (f, g) : Rp →Rm × Rn. It satisﬁes
F −1(0) = M ∩N and the kernel of its diﬀerential in u is
Ker TuF = Ker Tuf ∩Ker Tug,

PDF PAGE 573
A.3 Transversality
563
a subspace whose dimension is therefore
dim Ker TuF = dim Ker Tuf + dim Ker Tug −dim(Ker Tuf + Ker Tug)
= p −m + p −n −p = p −(m + n).
Consequently, TuF is surjective and F is a submersion in the neighborhood
of u, so that M ∩N is a submanifold. The proof also gives its tangent space,
namely the kernel of TuF, that is, the intersection of the tangent spaces, and
its codimension, which is the sum of the codimensions.
⊓⊔
Example A.3.2. The example of a surface M and a plane N in P = R3 is
already quite instructive. The two submanifolds are of codimension 1, so the
“general position” condition is simply that they are not tangent. A subman-
ifold of Rn and its (aﬃne) tangent space are never transverse.
We say that two submanifolds M and N of P are transverse at the point
u ∈P if
(
either u ̸∈M ∩N,
or u ∈M ∩N
and TuM + TuN = TuP.
We call them transverse, denoted by M ⋔N, if they are transverse at every
point.
Figure A.1 shows two submanifolds of R2 that are not transverse. . . and
the fact that they become so after a tiny deformation.
Remark A.3.3. This property is completely general: transversality is a
generic notion, it can always be attained through a small deformation. It
is also a stable notion: a small deformation of two transverse submanifolds
preserves their transversality. To prove the ﬁrst assertion, we can use Sard’s
theorem. Namely, since M and N are described by submersions f and g as
in the proof above, almost all values are regular for F, so that f −1(ε) and
g−1(η) are transverse for almost all (ε, η) near (0, 0).
Remark A.3.4. For two submanifolds whose dimensions add up to less than
the dimension of the surrounding manifold (for example two curves in R3),
transversality means the absence of any intersection.
More generally, if f : M →N is a diﬀerentiable map and if P ⊂N is a
submanifold, then we say that f is transverse to P (at u) if
(
either u ̸∈f(M) ∩P,
or u = f(x) ∈f(M) ∩P
and Txf(TxM) + TuP = TuN.
As before, we denote this by f ⋔P.

PDF PAGE 574
564
A A Bit of Diﬀerential Geometry
Fig. A.1
Proposition A.3.5. If f is transverse to P (at every point) and if f −1(P)
is nonempty, then f −1(P) is a submanifold of M.
⊓⊔
Remark A.3.6. If N ⊂P is a (co-orientable) orientable submanifold of the
orientable manifold P and if f : M →P is transverse to N, then f −1(N) is
(co-orientable) orientable in the orientable manifold M. The case treated in
Section A.1.h is that where the submanifold N is the point 0 and P = R.
A direct application of Sard’s theorem is the following ﬁrst result on
transversality.
Theorem A.3.7. Let M, N, and S be three manifolds and let P be a sub-
manifold of N. Let
F : M × S −→N
be a map transverse to P. Then for almost all s ∈S, the map Fs : M →N
is transverse to P.
We in fact see the manifold S as a parameter space.
Proof of Theorem A.3.7. By applying Proposition A.3.5, we see that F −1(P)
is a submanifold Q of M × S. The projection
π : Q ⊂M × S −→S
is a diﬀerentiable map for which, by Sard’s theorem, almost all s ∈S are
regular values. We can easily verify that the regular values of π are exactly
those s for which the map Fs is transverse to P.
⊓⊔
Remark A.3.8. The greater the parameter space S, the higher the prob-
ability that a map M × S →N is transverse to the submanifold P. This
explains the great usefulness of this result.

PDF PAGE 575
A.3 Transversality
565
A.3.b Ck Topology
The transversality theorems say that suﬃciently close to a given map, there
exists a map transverse to a given submanifold, or, that we can “perturb”
(“slightly” being implied) a map to make it transverse to a submanifold. To
state the theorems, we need to deﬁne a notion of proximity, that is, a topology,
or rather several, on the space of maps from manifold M to manifold N.
Let us begin with the case where the two manifolds are open subsets of
Euclidean spaces. The C∞(resp. Ck) topology is that of the uniform conver-
gence of the function and all of its derivatives (resp. those of order at most k)
on compact subsets.
Remark A.3.9. This topology is metrizable: we use the pseudo distances

source space.
In the general case of two manifolds M and N, we deﬁne the Ck topology
(where k may be inﬁnite) by giving a fundamental system of open neigh-
borhoods for it. For a function f, these neighborhoods are indexed by the
(K, V, ε) such that:
• ε > 0.
•
K is a compact subset of the open set of a chart of M.
•
V is the open set of a chart of N.
• And we have f(K) ⊂V .
We set

This topology is metrizable since M is assumed to be separable, like all
manifolds in this book. We will let d∞denote a distance deﬁning the C∞
topology.
A.3.c Transversality Theorems
Theorem A.3.10 (local transversality). Let M and N be two manifolds
and let P be a proper submanifold of N. Let f : M →N be a diﬀerentiable
map and let a be a point of M. Then there exist a compact neighborhood K
of a in M and an open neighborhood U of f in C∞(M, N) such that
{g ∈U | g ⋔P at every point of K}

566

is a dense open subset of U.
Theorem A.3.11 (transversality). Let K be a compact subset of M. The
set of maps from M to N transverse to the submanifold P of N along K is
a dense open subset of C∞(M, N).
Corollary A.3.12. The set of maps f : M →N transverse to the submani-
fold P of N is dense in C∞(M, N); moreover, it is open if M is compact.

It occasionally happens (and will occasionally happen) that we need a
somewhat diﬀerent transversality property: we do not want to consider all
the functions from M to N, but only those with a property speciﬁc to the
problem under consideration, which may not be an open property. In other
words, we want to consider only the functions satisfying some constraint.
Following [47], let us say that a subset F ⊂C∞(M, N) is locally transverse
to P if, for every function f ∈F and every a ∈M, there exist a neighbor-
hood K of a in M and a neighborhood V of f in F such that, for every g ∈V,
there exist a neighborhood U of 0 in a space Rq and a family
G : M × U −→N
such that
• G0 = g.
• For all t ∈U, Gt ∈F.
• G is transverse to P on K × U.
This deﬁnition adapts itself to most constraints, which in general do not
deﬁne open sets. It is used, for example, to prove the immersion theorem
and Whitney’s embedding theorem. Another advantage is that the proof of
the transversality theorem gives “transversality with constraints” without
needing any modiﬁcation.
Theorem A.3.13. If F ⊂C∞(M, N) is locally transverse to the submanifold
P ⊂N, then for every compact K ⊂M, the set of elements of F that are
transverse to P along K is a dense open subset of F.
⊓⊔

A.4 Vector Fields as Diﬀerential Equations
A.4.a Flows
A vector ﬁeld can be seen as a diﬀerential equation
c′(t) = Xc(t)
whose solution through x at t = 0 we denote by ϕt

ϕt

A very important result that we will use continually is the fact that on a
compact manifold, the ﬂow is deﬁned for all t.
Theorem A.4.1. The ﬂow of a vector ﬁeld on a compact manifold V is well
deﬁned on R × V .
The same of course holds for a vector ﬁeld with compact support on an
arbitrary manifold. See for example [54, p. 10] for a proof.
Collar Neighborhoods of the Boundary.
The boundary ∂V of a manifold V admits a “collar”4 neighborhood, that is,
a neighborhood of the form ∂V × [0, ε[, which we can obtain by integrating a
vector ﬁeld. Namely, we choose a vector ﬁeld deﬁned in the neighborhood of
the boundary and not tangent to it (this is easy to obtain using a partition
of unity) and integrate it.
A.4.b Lie Derivative, Cartans’ Formula
The Lie derivative LX extends the derivation of functions (0-forms). It is

A.5 Riemannian Metrics, Exponential Map
A Riemannian metric on a manifold is a section of the vector bundle of
bilinear forms on the tangent space to this manifold that at each point is an
inner product on the tangent space at that point.
For example, if V is a submanifold of Rn, then the Euclidean metric on
Rn induces an inner product on the vector spaces TxV and a Riemannian
metric on V .
When a manifold has a partition of unity, we can use it to endow the man-
ifold with a Riemannian metric; in particular, this applies to every compact
manifold.

PDF PAGE 583
A.5 Riemannian Metrics, Exponential Map
573
A Riemannian metric g deﬁnes a derivative of vector ﬁelds along a vector
ﬁeld, the covariant derivative called the Levi-Civit`a connection, the unique
(X, Y ) 7→DXY such that
DfXY = fDXY
and
DX(fY ) = (x · f)Y + fDXY
DXY −DY X = [X, Y ]
X · g(Y, Z) = g(DXY, Z) + g(Y, DXZ).
See [37, § II B].
On a Riemannian manifold, we therefore have the notion of a geodesic. A
geodesic is a solution t 7→γ(t) of the diﬀerential equation
D ˙γγ = 0.
Given a point x ∈V and a vector Y ∈TxV , there exists a unique geodesic
through x with tangent vector Y at x; that is,
γY (t)
with γY (0) = x and ˙γY (0) = Y.
The geodesics are solutions of a diﬀerential equation of order 2. We assume
that V is (geodesically) complete, namely that γY (t) is well deﬁned for every
t ∈R. We then deﬁne
expx : TxV −→V
Y 7−→γY (1).
We can show that expx is a local diﬀeomorphism and that
T0 expx : TxV −→TxV
is none other than the identity. See [37, § II C].
Since V is endowed with a Riemannian metric, we can consider, for ev-
ery R, the subspace
{(x, Y ) ∈TV | ∥Y ∥< R} ⊂TV.
We call this type of space a “disk bundle”: the ﬁber at x ∈V is the disk of
radius R in the Euclidean space TxV .
When the manifold V is compact, there exists a nonnegative real number r
such that the exponential deﬁnes a diﬀeomorphism
(x, Y ) 7−→(x, expx Y )

PDF PAGE 584
574
A A Bit of Diﬀerential Geometry
from the disk bundle of radius r
{(x, Y ) ∈TV | ∥Y ∥< r}
onto its image in V × V . The supremum of the set of such r is called an
injectivity radius.
With a Riemannian metric and its Levi-Civit`a connection is associated
a notion of “parallel transport”. Given a vector X tangent to V at x, we
deﬁne its parallel transport along a curve t 7→γ(t) with origin x = γ(0) as
follows: X(t) ∈Tγ(t)V is the unique “parallel” section, that is, section with
zero covariant derivative, of γ⋆TV satisfying the initial condition X(0) = X.

Appendix B
A Bit of Algebraic Topology
In a book devoted to (Morse, Floer) homology, we inevitably use some alge-
braic homology (K¨unneth formula, long exact sequences). But we also need
a minimum of algebraic topology (homotopy groups, ﬁrst Chern class,. . . ).
We take stock of all of this in the present chapter.

B.2 Chern Classes
Second Cohomology Group.
We have mentioned the ﬁrst Chern class of a complex vector bundle, even
though we have not used it much. It lives in the cohomology group H2(W; Z)
obtained, for example, using the Morse complex as in the ﬁrst part of this
book.
First Chern Class.
The most economical way to deﬁne it is by taking the maximal exterior power
of this ﬁber bundle, thus obtaining a complex line bundle. The ﬁrst Chern

PDF PAGE 590

B A Bit of Algebraic Topology
class is then the Euler class of the latter:
c1(E) = c1(ΛnE) = e(Λn(E)) ∈H2(W; Z)
if E is a ﬁber bundle of rank n on a space W. We refer to [56] for these
notions.
We have used the ﬁrst Chern class c1(TW) of the tangent bundle TW
of a symplectic manifold, seen as a complex vector bundle using an almost
complex structure calibrated by the symplectic form. Let us note that this
class is well deﬁned, simply because the set of such almost complex structures
is contractible (and in particular connected) and the Chern class c1 lives in
the discrete space H2(W; Z).

Appendix C
A Bit of Analysis
We ﬁrst recall the statement of the Arzel`a–Ascoli theorem. Then we set out
the basis of Fredholm theory. Next, we will prove some lemmas on elliptic
regularity, as well as the properties of ∂that we have used throughout this
text.
C.1 The Arzel`a–Ascoli Theorem
Let us recall this very useful theorem, which characterizes the relatively com-
pact subsets of the space of continuous functions on a compact space, and
which we use in several compactness statements. See [17] for this theorem
and for references.
Theorem C.1.1 (Arzel`a–Ascoli).
Let (E, d) be a compact metric space
and let F be a bounded family of continuous functions f : E →R that are
uniformly equicontinuous on E, that is, that satisfy
∀ε > 0, ∃δ > 0 such that ∀f ∈F,
d(x, y) < δ =⇒|f(x) −f(y)| < ε.
Then F is relatively compact in the space of continuous functions on E.
In other words, from every sequence of elements of F we can extract a
sequence that converges uniformly on E (to a continuous function).
Remark C.1.2. In all the cases where we applied this theorem in this book,
the family of functions was equicontinuous because it was “equi-Lipschitz”.
581

PDF PAGE 592
582
C A Bit of Analysis
C.2 Fredholm Theory
As is usually done and for the sake of simplicity, in this chapter we will call
an operator1 a continuous linear map from a normed vector space to another
(this is sometimes also called a bounded linear map).
C.2.a Fredholm Operators
We say that an operator L from a Banach space E to a Banach space F is
Fredholm if it has an index, namely if Ker L is a ﬁnite-dimensional subspace
and Im L is a ﬁnite-codimensional subspace (or Coker L is a ﬁnite-dimensional
space). The index of the operator is then
Ind(L) = dim Ker L −dim Coker L.
Examples C.2.1.
(1) A bijective operator has an index, namely zero.
(2) If E is a ﬁnite-dimensional vector space, then every linear map L : E →E
has index zero. . . since this assertion is equivalent to
dim Ker L + dim Im L = dim E.
Remark C.2.2. If L : E →F is a Fredholm operator, then Ind(L) is the
Euler characteristic of the chain complex
0 −→E
L
−−→F −→0
since in this simple case, the Euler characteristic is
χ = dim(Ker L/ {0}) −dim(F/ Im(L)).
The theory of Fredholm operators is explained very well in the talk by
Grisvard [41] that we have borrowed from shamelessly, hoping that we will
be forgiven for this borrowing because of the publicity it will bring to this
little-known article (we have no doubts about that). Here is a ﬁrst list of
properties that can all be veriﬁed directly.
Proposition C.2.3. Let L0 : E0 →F0, L1 : E1 →F1, L : E →F and
L′ : F →G be Fredholm operators. Then we have:
1 In the remainder of this book, we have occasionally used the term “operators” for dif-
ferentiable maps that are not necessarily linear and have also occasionally considered non-
continuous operators. As a rule, we always specify what the term refers to.

PDF PAGE 593
C.2 Fredholm Theory
583
(1) L0 ⊕L1 is a Fredholm operator and
Ind(L0 ⊕L1) = Ind(L0) + Ind(L1).
(2) If H is a vector space of ﬁnite dimension m, then L ⊗IdH is a Fredholm
operator, and index Ind(L ⊗IdH) = m Ind(L).
(3) L′ ◦L is a Fredholm operator and Ind(L′ ◦L) = Ind(L′) + Ind(L).
Proof. The ﬁrst assertion is clear and the second one follows from the ﬁrst
by choosing a basis for H and writing
E ⊗H ∼= E ⊕· · · ⊕E
and
L ⊗IdH = L ⊕· · · ⊕L.
By applying Remark C.2.2, we could also have considered the short exact
sequence of chain complexes

Proposition C.2.4. Let L : E →F be an operator whose kernel has ﬁnite
dimension and whose image has ﬁnite codimension; then its image is closed.
Proof. Let H be a closed subspace3 of E such that Ker(L) ⊕H = E and G
be a ﬁnite-dimensional subspace of F such that Im(L) ⊕G = F. Consider
the linear map
Γ : H ⊕G −→F
(x, y) 7−→L(x) + y.
Since G has ﬁnite dimension, this map is continuous. Moreover, it is easy to
check that it is bijective. Using Banach’s theorem, its inverse is also continu-
ous and therefore its image Γ(H ⊕0) must be closed. But Γ(H ⊕0) = Im(L)
and the proof is ﬁnished.
⊓⊔
C.2.b Basic Properties
Proposition C.2.5. Let L : E →F be an operator between two Banach
spaces. For L to be Fredholm, it is necessary and suﬃcient that there exists
an operator L′ : F →E such that L ◦L′ −IdF and L′ ◦L −IdE have ﬁnite
3 This is a simple consequence of the Hahn–Banach theorem.

PDF PAGE 595
C.2 Fredholm Theory
585
rank. When this is the case, L′ is also a Fredholm operator and
Ind(L′) = −Ind(L).
Proof. To begin, let us assume that L is a Fredholm operator. We choose a
complement E0 of Ker L in E, so that the restriction L0 of L to E0 is an
isomorphism from E0 onto Im(L). We consider the inverse L−1
0
of L0. We
also choose a projection p : F →Im(L) that we take to be continuous, and
we set
L′ = L−1
0
◦p;
then L′ is continuous. We have
Im(L′ ◦L −IdE) = Ker(L) and Im(L ◦L′ −IdF ) = Ker p,
which are both ﬁnite-dimensional. We also have
L ◦L′ ◦L = L
and
L′ ◦L ◦L′ = L′.
Conversely, if L′ has the two properties, then let us show that L and L′
are Fredholm operators. Let A = L ◦L′ −IdF and let B = L′ ◦L −IdE. We
have
Ker(L) ⊂Ker(L′ ◦L) ⊂Im(B)
(the ﬁrst kernel is therefore ﬁnite-dimensional) and
Im(L) ⊃Im(L ◦L′) = Im(IdF +A).
If G = Ker(A), then F/G is isomorphic to Im(A), hence ﬁnite-dimensional.
Therefore G has ﬁnite codimension. However, IdF +A equals IdF on Ker(A),
hence Im(IdF +A) ⊃G, so that the image indeed has ﬁnite codimension.
So L has an index, as does L′. The formula for the index follows from the
additivity formula of Proposition C.2.3 and from the following proposition,
which will imply that the index of IdF +A is zero.
⊓⊔
Proposition C.2.6. If u : E →E is an operator of ﬁnite rank, then
Ind(IdE +u) = 0.
If L : E →F is a Fredholm operator and if u : E →F is an operator of ﬁnite
rank, then L + u is Fredholm and its index is
Ind(L + u) = Ind(L).

Proof. The ﬁrst assertion is a computation of dimensions, which we can deal

always zero.

that

Proposition C.2.7. If K : E →E is a compact operator on a Banach space,
then IdE +K is a Fredholm operator.
Proof. We consider L = IdE +K : E →E. First, IdE |Ker(L) = −K|Ker(L), so
IdE |Ker(L) is a compact operator, which means that the unit ball of Ker(L)
is compact, and therefore that Ker(L) is ﬁnite-dimensional.
Let us now show that the image of L is closed. We choose a complement E0
of Ker(L) in E and show that there exists a constant C > 0 such that
∀x ∈E0,
∥x∥≤C ∥L(x)∥.

PDF PAGE 597
C.2 Fredholm Theory
587
Indeed, if this were not the case, then we would be able to ﬁnd a sequence
(xk) of elements of norm 1 in E0 such that
lim
k→+∞(xk + K(xk)) = 0.
Since K is compact, we would then be able to extract a sequence such that
(K(xk)) converges. But then (xk) would also converge. The limit x would
then have the following properties
∥x∥= 1,
x ∈E0 ∩Ker(L),
which is absurd.
We therefore have the desired inequality, which implies that the image of L
is closed: if (xk) is a sequence in E such that
lim
k→+∞L(xk) = y,
then (xk) is a Cauchy sequence and therefore converges to a vector of E
whose image is y.
Finally, we consider the transposes E′ →E′ (here E′ denotes the dual
of E, endowed with the weak topology4). The operator tK is compact, hence
we can use the same argument as for Ker(L) to show that the dimension of
Ker(tL) is ﬁnite. Together with the fact that Im(L) is closed, this concludes
the proof of the proposition.
⊓⊔
From these propositions, we deduce three results that are essential for the
theory of Fredholm operators.
Theorem C.2.8. Let L : E →F be an operator between Banach spaces.
For L to be a Fredholm operator, it is necessary and suﬃcient that there
exists an L′ : F →E such that
L ◦L′ −IdF
and
L′ ◦L −IdE
are compact operators.
Proof. Operators of ﬁnite rank are compact, so Proposition C.2.5 gives the
necessity of the condition. Next, we have
Ker(L) ⊂Ker(L′ ◦L)
and
Im(L) ⊃Im(L ◦L′)
so that the result follows from the previous proposition.
⊓⊔
4 We always have that Ker tL is the annihilator of Im(L); we do not always have that
Im(L) is the annihilator of Ker tL, but the latter is true if Im(L) is closed.

PDF PAGE 598
588
C A Bit of Analysis
Theorem C.2.9 (invariance of the index under small perturbations).
Let L : E →F be a Fredholm operator between two Banach spaces. There
exists a constant ε > 0 such that, for every operator u : E →F of norm
∥u∥< ε, the operator L + u is Fredholm and
Ind(L + u) = Ind(L).
Proof. We use an operator L′ as given by Proposition C.2.5 and set
ε =
1
∥L′∥.
Hence, for ∥u∥< ε, the operators IdF +u ◦L′ and IdE +L′ ◦u are invertible.
In particular, their indices are zero and we have
(L + u) ◦L′ = L ◦L′ + u ◦L′ = IdF +A + u ◦L′
for an operator A of ﬁnite rank. Since IdF +u ◦L′ is invertible, the operator
IdF +A + u ◦L′ has index zero (by Proposition C.2.6). Since
Im((L + u) ◦L′) ⊂Im(L + u),
this image has ﬁnite codimension. We can show in the same manner (using
L′ ◦L = IdE +B with B of ﬁnite rank) that Ker(L + u) is ﬁnite-dimensional.
Finally, we have on the one hand,
Ind(L + u) + Ind(L′) = 0,
and on the other hand,
Ind(L) + Ind(L′) = 0,
whence it follows that
Ind(L + u) = Ind(L).
⊓⊔
Theorem C.2.10. Let L : E →F be a Fredholm operator between two Ba-
nach spaces. For every compact operator K : E →F, the operator L + K is
Fredholm and
Ind(L + K) = Ind(L).
Proof. We still use the operator L′ from Proposition C.2.5; the operators
(L + K) ◦L′ −IdF
and
L′ ◦(L + K) −IdE

PDF PAGE 599
C.2 Fredholm Theory
589
are compact, so that L + K is Fredholm (Theorem C.2.8). The same holds
for L + tK for every t ∈[0, 1], so that, by Theorem C.2.9, we have
Ind(L + K) = Ind(L).
⊓⊔
Remark C.2.11. Theorem C.2.9 asserts that the index is a continuous map
on the set of Fredholm operators, which is an open subset of the space of
operators from E to F.
Remark C.2.12. The operator IdE +K, which by Proposition C.2.7 is Fred-
holm, therefore has index zero.
C.2.c Another Useful Result
To prove that the linearized operator L of the Floer equation is a Fredholm
operator, we used Proposition 8.7.4, which we repeat here.
Proposition ( 8.7.4). Let E, F and G be three Banach spaces. Let L : E →
F be an operator and let K : E →G be a compact operator. We suppose that
there exists a constant C > 0 such that
∀x ∈E,
∥x∥E ≤C (∥L(x)∥F + ∥K(x)∥G) .
Then the kernel of L is ﬁnite-dimensional and the image of L is closed.
Proof. The proof is inspired by that of Proposition C.2.7. Let us ﬁrst show
that Ker L is a ﬁnite-dimensional subspace of E. To do this, let us show that
its unit ball is compact. Let (xk) be a sequence of elements of this ball:
∥xk∥E ≤1
and
L(xk) = 0.
The hypothesis is that
∥xk∥E ≤C ∥K(xk)∥.
The image of the unit ball under K is relatively compact, hence after extract-
ing a subsequence, if necessary, we may, and do, assume that the sequence
(K(xk)) converges in G. This implies that (xk) is a Cauchy sequence, whence,
since E is complete, that it converges in E and therefore in the unit ball. This
proves the compactness of the unit ball.
Let us now show that Im(L) is a closed subspace of F. Let (xk) be a
sequence of elements of E such that the sequence (L(xk)) converges to an
element y ∈F and let us show that y belongs to the image of L:
• If the sequence (xk) is bounded, then the inequality gives
∥xk∥E ≤C (∥L(xk)∥F + ∥K(xk)∥G) ,

PDF PAGE 600
590
C A Bit of Analysis
where L(xk) →y in F and K(xk) remains in a compact subset of G. It
follows that (xk) admits a subsequence that is a Cauchy sequence and
therefore converges. Its limit x satisﬁes y = L(x).
• If the sequence (xk) is not bounded, then let us show that we obtain a
contradiction. In this case, the sequence admits a subsequence with
lim
k→+∞∥xk∥= +∞.
Since Ker L is a ﬁnite dimensional subspace, we can ﬁnd a complement E0
and we may, and do, assume that xk ∈E0 for every k. Let
uk =
xk
∥xk∥E
∈E0.
Our inequality gives
∥uk∥E ≤C (∥L(uk)∥F + ∥K(uk)∥G) .
Once more, the sequence (K(uk)) admits a convergent subsequence. Here
the sequence (L(uk)) converges to 0, hence we may, and do, assume that
(uk) is a Cauchy sequence and therefore converges in E0 to a limit
u ∈Ker L ∩E0 = {0} .
Consequently u = 0 though it should have norm 1, giving the expected
contradiction.
⊓⊔
C.2.d Fredholm Maps
We call a diﬀerentiable map
F : E −→F
from one Banach space to another a Fredholm map if at every point it has a
(continuous) diﬀerential
TxF : E −→F
that is moreover a Fredholm operator. Let us note that in this case the index
of TxF does not depend on x (by Theorem C.2.9 and Remark C.2.11). We
therefore denote it by Ind(F).
The local inversion theorem in Banach spaces (see [19]) allows us to prove
the following result, as in the ﬁnite-dimensional case.

PDF PAGE 601
C.3 Distribution Spaces, Weak Solutions
591
Theorem C.2.13. Let F : E →F be a Fredholm map and let y ∈F be such
that
∀x ∈F−1(y),
TxF : E −→F
is surjective. Then F−1(y) is a manifold of dimension Ind(F) and its tangent
space at x is Ker TxF.
C.3 Distribution Spaces, Weak Solutions
C.3.a Distributions
We quickly recall the basic deﬁnitions of distribution theory. We refer the
readers to their favorite book. As for us, we used [13].
For every open subset U of Rn, we consider the space C∞
0 (U) of functions
of class C∞with compact support in U. The space D′(U) of distributions
on U is a dual of C∞
0 (U). More precisely, it is the space of linear functionals
on C∞
0 (U) that are continuous in the sense that for every compact subset
K ⊂U, there exist an integer p and a constant C such that
for every ϕ ∈C∞
0 (U) with support in K,
|⟨u, ϕ⟩| ≤C sup
x∈K
|α|≤p

∂xα (x)
.
We say that a sequence un of distributions converges to a linear func-
tional u if we have that
for every ϕ ∈C∞
0 (U),
lim
n→+∞⟨un, ϕ⟩= ⟨u, ϕ⟩.
It is not entirely trivial, but the limit u is then also a distribution.
We deﬁne the support of a distribution in an obvious manner (as the
complement of the greatest open set restricted to which the distribution is
zero) and we let E′(U) denote the space of distributions with compact support
in U.
Examples.
(1) Taking the value of functions at a given point of U deﬁnes a linear func-
tional on C∞
0 (U) that is a distribution, namely
⟨δa, ϕ⟩= ϕ(a),
the Dirac delta function at a (we write δ = δ0).

PDF PAGE 602
592
C A Bit of Analysis
(2) More generally, linear combinations of partial derivatives (such as ∂, ∆)
taken at a point a are also distributions.
(3) If f is a locally integrable function on U, then for every ϕ ∈C∞
0 (U), we
can deﬁne the linear functional
⟨f, ϕ⟩=
Z
fϕ dx,
which is a distribution. If two locally integrable functions f and g are
equal almost everywhere on U, then they deﬁne the same linear functional.
Moreover, if the sequence (fn) converges to f for the L1 norm on the
compact subsets of U, then it tends to f in the sense of distributions, so
that L1
loc is consequently a subspace of D′(U). The same holds for Lp
loc
(with H¨older’s inequality).

C.3.c Convolution
By copying the deﬁnition of the convolution of two functions (let us take u
integrable and ϕ of class C∞),
(u ⋆ϕ)(x) =
Z
ϕ(x −y)u(y) dy = ⟨u(y), ϕ(x −y)⟩,
we can convolute a distribution with compact support and a C∞function
with compact support by setting, for u ∈E′(Rn), for every ϕ ∈C∞
0 (Rn) and
at every point x,
(u ⋆ϕ)(x) = ⟨u(y), ϕ(x −y)⟩.

C.3 Distribution Spaces, Weak Solutions
593
We refer, for example, to the course [13] for all properties of convolution
and in particular for those that follow. The function u⋆ϕ thus deﬁned belongs
to C∞
0 (Rn), its support satisﬁes
Supp(u ⋆ϕ) ⊂Supp u + Supp ϕ,
and its derivatives satisfy
∂α
∂xα (u ⋆ϕ) = u ⋆
∂αϕ
∂xα

=
∂αu
∂xα

⋆ϕ.
If u is a distribution with compact support and ϕ and ψ are two C∞functions
with compact support, then we have the following associativity property:
(u ⋆ϕ) ⋆ψ = u ⋆(ϕ ⋆ψ).
More generally, we can convolute distributions, for example two functions
in L1
loc whose supports A and B are “convolutive”, that is, such that
∀R > 0, ∃ρ(R) > 0 such that
(x ∈A, y ∈B, ∥x + y∥≤R) =⇒(∥x∥≤ρ(R), ∥y∥≤ρ(R)).
This is a commutative (and associative) product.
In this way, we can deﬁne the convolution of a distribution in E′(U) and a
C∞function, or of an arbitrary distribution and a C∞function with compact
support. In both cases, the result is a C∞function.
Another useful remark is the fact that δ, the Dirac delta function at 0, is
an identity for this “product”:
δ ⋆ϕ = ϕ
(this follows from the deﬁnition of convolution).
C.3.d Fundamental Solution, Weak Solutions
Let L be a diﬀerential operator. If f is a function in Lp
loc (or more generally a
distribution), then we call weak solution of the equation Lu = f a solution in
the space of distributions. If L has constant coeﬃcients, then the properties
of convolution given above imply that Lu = δ ⋆Lu = Lδ ⋆u, so that the
equation can also be written as Lδ ⋆u = f. We call fundamental solution of
the equation Lu = 0, or “fundamental solution of L”, a distribution E that
satisﬁes
Lδ ⋆E = L(E) = δ,

PDF PAGE 604
594
C A Bit of Analysis
where Lδ is seen as a distribution.
For example, by Cauchy’s formula, this is the case for the function 1/πz
when L is the operator ∂, as we will prove in Section C.5.
Formally, if E is a fundamental solution of L, then we may hope to have
Lδ ⋆(E ⋆f) = (Lδ ⋆E) ⋆f = δ ⋆f = f
for every function f, and therefore to be able to express the solutions of
Lu = f through convolution with a fundamental solution. This is indeed the
case. More precisely, we have the following result.
Theorem C.3.1 ([13, Theorem 8.1.2]). If L is a diﬀerential operator with
constant coeﬃcients that admits a fundamental solution E and if f is a dis-
tribution with compact support, then the equation Lu = f admits a unique
weak solution with compact support, namely u = E ⋆f.
Proof. The argument above shows that u = E ⋆f is indeed a solution. The
uniqueness of this solution comes from the fact that it has compact support:
if u is a solution with compact support then the supports of u, Lδ and E
satisfy the “convolutivity” condition and the resulting associativity gives
u = δ ⋆u = (E ⋆(Lδ)) ⋆u = E ⋆((Lδ) ⋆u) = E ⋆f.
⊓⊔
C.4 Sobolev Spaces on Rn
Let us recall here the deﬁnition of Sobolev spaces. We let U denote an open

The C∞functions are dense, in the following precise sense.
Proposition C.4.4 (see [17, Section 9.3]). Let U be an open subset of Rn
whose boundary is a submanifold of class C1. Then the restrictions to U of the
C∞functions with compact support on Rn form a dense subspace of W 1,p(U).
Relations Between Sobolev Spaces

The following theorems bring together the Sobolev estimates and a com-
pactness statement called Rellich’s theorem.
Theorem C.4.9 (see [17, Section 9.3]). Let U be a bounded open subset
of Rn whose boundary is of class C1. Let k ∈N, k ≥1, and let p satisfy
1 < p < +∞. Then we have:
(1) If p < n/k, then W k,p(U) ⊂Lq(U) with q = np/(n −kp).
(2) If p = n/k, then W k,p(U) ⊂Lq(U) for every q ≥p.
(3) If p > n/k, then W k,p(U) ⊂L∞(U).
And the inclusions are all continuous. Moreover, for u ∈W k,p(U), we have

Being of class C2 is not an essential condition for the harmonicity, as
expressed by the following lemma (often called “Weyl’s lemma”).
Lemma C.5.4. Every weak solution u ∈Lp
loc(U) (for p ≥1) of ∆u = 0 is

∆(u ⋆βδ) = βδ ⋆∆u
(by deﬁnition of the weak derivatives), so that u ⋆βδ is harmonic if u is a
weak solution. In particular, for every δ, the convolution u ⋆βδ satisﬁes the
mean value equality.
However, u ⋆βδ converges to u for the Lp norm and almost everywhere on
the compact subsets of U when δ tends to 0. So the weak solution u also sat-
isﬁes the mean value property, and therefore is of class C∞and consequently
harmonic by Lemma C.5.3.
⊓⊔
The real and imaginary parts of a weak solution of the Cauchy–Riemann
equation are the weak solutions of the Laplace equations. We therefore have
the following result.
Lemma C.5.5. Every weak solution u of the Cauchy–Riemann equation that
is locally integrable over an open set U is holomorphic.

We can easily verify5 that K belongs to L1

of Theorem C.3.1.
Proposition C.5.7. Let u and f be two functions with compact support
on R2. We suppose that u, f ∈L1(R2). Then the function u is a weak
solution of ∆u = f if and only if u = K ⋆f.
